How to Loop Your Transformer: Loop Scaling,
Specialization, and Parallel Inference
Chanakya Ekbote1,2,†, Artidoro Pagnoni1,∗, Devendra Singh Sachan1, Shruti Bhosale1, Mike Lewis1,
Maryam Fazel-Zarandi1, Shashank Rajput1,∗, Sujan Kumar Gonugondla1,∗
1FAIR at Meta, 2MIT
†Work done at FAIR/Meta, ∗Equal contribution
Looping in transformer models reuses weights by repeatedly applying the same transformer, or parts of
it, to token representations, effectively making the model deeper without increasing its parameter count.
Whether looping is useful depends on which constraint dominates: model size, training compute, or
autoregressive inference efficiency. We study these trade-offs jointly in dense decoder-only transformers
trained from scratch, varying which parts of the model are looped, how many loops are applied, and
how information is passed between loops. Our experiments span models with up to 1B parameters,
trained on up to 155B tokens using up to 1.07 × 1021 training FLOPs, with up to 32 loops. We find
that looping the full transformer generally gives the strongest quality at a fixed parameter budget,
whereas looping a middle block of layers gives a better return when training FLOPs are constrained.
These trade-offs also depend on the number of loops: adding loops is not uniformly beneficial, and
gains eventually saturate, reverse, or become unstable. This saturation motivates asking whether
forcing every loop to use exactly the same weights limits the benefit of deeper looping. We address this
by adding small loop-specific low-rank adapters that improve quality, with larger gains as more loops
are added. A separate practical concern for looped transformers is the sequential dependency during
autoregressive generation, which causes a slowdown proportional to the number of loops. Parallel
Loop Transformers address this limitation by passing information between loops across adjacent token
positions via hidden states, allowing loop stages to run concurrently during decoding. However, in
our experiments the original formulation does not consistently retain the quality gains of ordinary
looping. We find that preserving the current-token embedding and separately normalizing it and the
previous-token hidden state before combining them recovers strong performance across model scales
and training budgets. Together, these results show that effective looping requires matching the design
to the resource constraint and inference setting.
Date: October 2026
Correspondence: Sujan Kumar Gonugondla at sujan@meta.com
1
Introduction
Looping in transformer models reuses weights by repeatedly applying the same transformer, or selected parts
of it, to token representations, allowing the model to behave like a deeper network without storing a new set
of parameters at every ‘depth’ (Dehghani et al., 2018; Saunshi et al., 2025; Geiping et al., 2025). This makes
looping attractive when model size is constrained, but the additional depth is not free: every extra loop must
still be executed. As a result, a design that uses parameters efficiently may not use training compute efficiently.
Prior work reflects this trade-off, showing that repeated shared computation can recover some of the benefits
of additional depth, while adding independently parameterized layers can be preferable when training compute
is constrained (Schwethelm et al., 2026; Prairie et al., 2026). The same weight reuse, however, raises another
question as more loops are added. In ordinary looping, every loop applies the same weights, even though later
loops operate on representations that have already been transformed several times. This suggests that exact
weight sharing may give diminishing returns with deeper looping. Prior work has relaxed this constraint by
giving each loop iteration a different low-rank adapter while keeping the main transformer weights shared
(Bae et al., 2025).

Figure 1 Looping can improve data and compute efficiency (in over-trained regimes) while retaining downstream quality. We
compare Vanilla (no looping), Standard Looping (repeatedly applying the same Transformer sequentially), and our
modified versions Parallel Loop Transformer (PLT) (Wu et al., 2025) and Looped Transformers. Left: for 1B models
trained across TPP 10–160, all looping methods achieve a given DCLM (Li et al., 2024) bits-per-byte (BPB) value with
fewer training tokens than the fitted Vanilla baseline, with the modified PLT variants providing the largest relative
data-efficiency gains. Center: across model sizes from 100M to 1B, we compare Vanilla models trained at TPP = 160
with two-loop models trained at TPP = 80. All looping methods achieve a given DCLM BPB with fewer training
FLOPs than the fitted Vanilla baseline, and the modified PLT and Looped variants are more efficient than Standard
Looping. Right: for 1B models across TPP 10–160, looping retains or improves mean NLL-ranked accuracy across
eight reasoning and question-answering tasks; the modified PLT variants match or exceed Standard Looping. See
Appendix D for the efficiency calculation and task-level results.
Looped transformers also suffer from a separate issue during autoregressive generation: each loop for a token
still depends on the output of the previous loop, so the model must execute them one after another, increasing
the sequential computation required to generate each token. Parallel Loop Transformers (PLTs) reorganize
this dependency by passing hidden states from one loop to the next across adjacent token positions, allowing
multiple loop stages to be evaluated in parallel during decoding (Wu et al., 2025). This change alters the
information available to each loop, and recent work reports that gains can decrease as more parallel loops are
added (Yang et al., 2026). This leaves open what information the shifted transition must preserve for parallel
looping to remain effective at greater loop depth. Together, these studies show that looping is not a single
design choice but a set of coupled trade-offs. Repeating more of the model can make better use of a fixed
parameter budget but requires more training compute; reusing the same weights across more loops can make
exact sharing increasingly restrictive; and changing the dependency between loops can parallelize sequential
looping while also changing the information available to each loop. These trade-offs may also change with
model scale and training budget. Despite prior work studying these choices in different settings, it remains
unclear which conclusions generalize and how the choices interact. We therefore study them within a common
training from-scratch setting across model scales and training budgets, and ask:
How should transformer layers be looped under constraints on model size, training compute, and
autoregressive inference?
We address this question through a controlled study of dense decoder-only transformers trained from scratch.
Our experiments span models with up to 1B parameters, up to 155B training tokens, up to 1.07 × 1021 training
FLOPs, and up to 32 loops. We organize the study around three linked aspects of looping:
Main Contributions.
1. We characterize where and how much to loop. We compare different looping designs and vary the
number of loops across each design. We find that looping the full transformer gives the strongest
quality at a fixed parameter budget, whereas looping a contiguous block of transformers layers in
the middle of the network, gives a better return when training FLOPs are constrained. We also find
that adding more loops is not uniformly beneficial: gains eventually saturate, reverse, or become
unstable (subsection 3.2).
2. We show when loop-specific specialization becomes useful. We compare exact weight sharing across
2


loops with small loop-specific low-rank adapters that allow each loop to learn a slightly different
transformation. These adapters provide only modest gains when few loops are used, but become
consistently more useful as the same weights are reused across more loops (subsection 3.3).
3. We present small modifications that improve stacked looping and PLTs We study Parallel Loop
Transformers (PLTs) (Wu et al., 2025), which change the dependency between loops so that their
computations can be parallelized during autoregressive decoding. Naively applying PLT in our
setting does not preserve the gains from looping: quality can degrade as more loops are added. We
find that preserving the current-token representation, separately normalizing it and the recurrent
state, and controlling how they are combined substantially improves PLT. With these changes,
parallel looping can outperform looping the full transformer while retaining a parallelizable decoding
dependency. Applying similar changes to standard looping improves full-transformer looping further,
showing that the modification is useful beyond the parallel setting (subsection 3.4).
2
Background and Methods
2.1
Different Ways to Loop a Transformer
We consider several ways to loop a transformer by varying which parts of the model are looped and the order
in which the loops are applied. In Full-Stack Looping, the output of the final transformer block is fed back to
the first block, so the entire stack of transformer blocks is applied again at each loop. In Per-Layer Looping,
the output of each transformer block is fed back to the same block for all loops before moving to the next
transformer block. We also consider looping only a contiguous set of transformer blocks in the middle of the
model. In Middle-Stack Looping, the output of the last block in this region is fed back to the first, so the
middle blocks are looped as a group. In Middle-Layer Looping, each transformer block in the middle region is
looped before moving to the next transformer block. The transformer blocks outside this region are applied
once. Finally, we vary which part of a transformer block is looped: the full block, attention only, the MLP
only, or attention and the MLP independently. See Figure 2 and subsection B.1 for details.
2.2
Loop-Specific Specialization
In standard transformer looping, every loop uses the same transformer weights. This keeps parameter storage
low, but may limit what later loops can learn, since they operate on representations already transformed by
earlier loops. Giving each loop a separate set of weights would remove this constraint, but would also give up
much of the parameter savings from looping. We instead use Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) (Hu et al., 2021)
to give each loop a small loop-specific adapter while keeping the main transformer weights shared, as defined
in Def. 1. We refer to this as loop-specific specialization.
Definition 1:
Loop-Specific Low-Rank Adapter. For a weight matrix W ∈Rdout×din shared across loops,
loop ℓuses W (ℓ) = W + α
r B(ℓ)A(ℓ), where A(ℓ) ∈Rr×din and B(ℓ) ∈Rdout×r are learned specifically
for loop ℓ.
We also evaluate alternative ways of parameterizing and combining these loop-specific low-rank adapters; see
Figure 4 and subsection B.2 for details.
2.3
Parallelizing Loops During Inference
In ordinary looping, each token is processed through each loop one after another: loop ℓreceives the
representation of that same token produced by loop ℓ−1. As a result, loop ℓcannot begin until loop ℓ−1 has
finished. Parallel Loop Transformers (PLTs) (Wu et al., 2025) change this dependency through Cross-Loop
Parallelism (CLP). Instead of passing the hidden state between loops at the same token position, CLP passes
the state from the previous loop at the preceding token position. As these previous-token states are already
available during autoregressive decoding, all the loop computations for the current token can be evaluated
simultaneously. To make this dependency (CLP) precise, let en denote the embedding of token n, let b(ℓ)
n
3


and h(ℓ)
n
denote the input and output of loop ℓ, and let Fθ denote the transformer whose weights are shared
across loops.
Definition 2:
Cross-Loop Parallelism (CLP). The first loop receives the current-token embedding,
b(1)
n
= en. Each later loop receives the current-token embedding together with the state produced by
the previous loop for the preceding token:
b(ℓ)
n = en + h(ℓ−1)



.
The original PLT combines CLP with two mechanisms that reduce KV-cache memory: KV sharing and
(G-SWA). KV sharing lets later loops reuse the full KV cache produced by the first loop instead of storing a
separate full cache for every loop. Each later loop also keeps a small local sliding-window cache, and gated
sliding-window attention (G-SWA) combines the attention outputs from the shared and local caches. In our
experiments, we separate CLP, KV sharing, and G-SWA to study their effects independently. See Figure 6
and subsection B.3 for details.
3
Experiments
3.1
Experimental Setup
We train models with 70M, 100M, 200M, 300M, 500M, and 1B parameters, containing 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, and
16 stored transformer layers, respectively. Training budgets range from 10 to 160 tokens per parameter
(TPP). All models use the same pretraining mixture of natural language, code, mathematical, and scientific
content, with the data sources, sampling distribution, preprocessing, tokenizer, and training setup held
fixed across architectures. We use a fixed subset of the DCLM Baseline 1.0 validation set (Li et al., 2024),
containing approximately 41 million tokens, as our primary measure of language-modeling quality and report
bits per byte (BPB; lower is better). To test whether the trends observed on DCLM hold across datasets, we
additionally evaluate on other language-modeling corpora and a broader set of mathematical, code, reasoning,
and question-answering benchmarks. These results, together with the full model configurations, training
details, evaluation protocols, and task-level results, are provided in the appendix (see Appendix C).
3.2
Which Forms of Looping Work, and When?
While keeping the model size fixed, there is a large design space for deciding which components of the
transformer to loop, in what order, and how many times to loop them. We investigate which of these choices
improve model quality and whether their effects depend on model scale and training budget. We compare seven
designs (Figure 2) across five dense model scales ranging from 70M to 500M parameters, token-to-parameter
ratios (TPP) of up to 160, and loop factors L ∈{2, 4, 8, 16, 32}, where available. At each model size, we
consider experiments that constrain either (a) the total amount of training data (i.e., fixed TPP) or (b) the
total training compute (i.e., a fixed FLOP budget). Our primary metric is DCLM validation bits per byte
(BPB; lower is better). Figure 3 shows the results at the 100M and 500M scales. Complete sweeps and
targeted 1B comparisons appear in Appendix E.
Benefits and limits of looping.
Using a moderate loop factor (L ≤16), looping improves quality across the
evaluated designs, model scales, and TPP settings: relative to the corresponding no-looping model, increasing
the loop factor L reduces DCLM BPB (Figure 3). However, the marginal gains diminish as L increases, and
performance eventually degrades at very large L. This effect is clearest in the 70M and 100M sweeps, which
extend to L = 32. The point at which these gains saturate or reverse depends on both the looping strategy
and TPP. At higher TPP, increasing L produces more consistent gains, whereas lower-TPP runs tend to
saturate or regress sooner. The strategy that loops attention and the MLP independently degrades sharply
as L increases, whereas Full-Stack Looping and Middle-Stack Looping avoid regression over a wider range
of L (Figure 13). Moreover, the relative performance of the looping strategies changes with model size: the
benefits of looping persist as model size increases, while the strongest strategies become more consistent. At
4


Figure 2 Looping designs: what is repeated, where, and in what order. Top: the looped component can be the complete
transformer block, attention alone, the MLP alone, or attention and the MLP separately. Bottom: full-stack looping
traverses the complete transformer for L loops, whereas per-layer looping completes L loops of each block before
advancing to the next. Middle-stack and middle-layer looping restrict the corresponding schedule to the central blocks.
For a model containing B blocks, the first and last ⌊B/3⌋blocks are run once.
100M and 200M, Per-Layer Looping and Middle-Stack Looping can be competitive with Full-Stack Looping
when a smaller L ≤2 is used, depending on TPP. At 300M and especially 500M, Full-Stack Looping and
Middle-Stack Looping perform well across TPP settings and loop factors, with Full-Stack Looping achieving
the lowest BPB as TPP increases (Figure 14). The 200M–500M sweeps extend only to L = 8 and 1B sweeps
up to L = 4, because the computational cost of looping grows linearly with L. Within these ranges, we still
see diminishing relative improvements with L (see Figure 45 and Figure 46 for 1B ablations). Comparing
performance as a function of total training FLOPs shows that some looping strategies use compute more
effectively than others. At lower training FLOP budgets, Middle-Stack Looping provides the best return on
additional compute, but when compute is not a constraint, Full-Stack Looping can achieve lower absolute
BPB, as shown in Figure 3 and Figure 46.
Note that similar trends hold across additional model scales and evaluation datasets (Appendix E). The
diminishing returns from adding more loops raise a natural question: does sharing exactly the same weights
across loops limit further improvements? We investigate this question in subsection 3.3.
3.3
When Should Loops Specialize?
We now investigate whether requiring every loop to reuse the same weights limits model quality. To do so, we
explore a middle ground between complete weight sharing in a standard looped model and using unique weights
at every depth in a deeper model. For our primary experiments, we use Full-Stack Looping because it generally
provides the strongest absolute improvements. However, the proposed approach applies to any looping design,
and we evaluate it with other designs in Appendix F. Figure 4 illustrates three ways to introduce loop-specific
adapters. Given a weight matrix W shared across L loops, loop ℓ∈{1, . . . , L} uses a loop-specific weight
defined as W (ℓ) = W + ∆W (ℓ). We use different designs for ∆W (ℓ): Loop LoRA assigns each loop its own
low-rank adapter, ∆W (ℓ) = BℓAℓ. Loop Matrix learns an unconstrained mixing matrix M ∈RL×L, where
Mℓj controls the contribution of low-rank component j to loop ℓ: ∆W (ℓ) = PL
j=1 MℓjBjAj. Loop Softmax
parameterizes these coefficients using a learned score matrix S ∈RL×L, with Mℓj = softmax(Sℓ,:)j. This
makes the coefficients nonnegative and normalizes them to sum to one. Loop Matrix and Loop Softmax can
combine several low-rank components, increasing the effective rank and expressivity of each loop-specific
adapter; see Appendix F. For these experiments, each low-rank component has rank at most 8. We use
unit scaling and jointly train the shared weights and loop-specific adapters from initialization, and apply

Figure 3 Full-Stack Looping is favored under fixed parameter-count and training-data budgets, whereas Middle-Stack Looping is
favored under FLOP constraints. DCLM validation BPB versus the number of loops or total training FLOPs for 100M
and 500M models. Each panel holds the model size and training-data budget constant, so the differences reflect how
each strategy uses the additional computation introduced by looping. The advantage of full-stack looping becomes
clearer at larger model scales and training budgets. Middle-Stack Looping with fewer loops achieves lower BPB when
compute is limited, whereas Full-Stack Looping can attain lower BPB when more compute is available. Increasing the
number of loops does not improve quality indefinitely: the 100M results exhibit saturation, regression, and instability
when more loops are used. Lower BPB is better; red triangles indicate unstable or clipped runs.
the updates to the attention and MLP weights in every transformer block. As in the preceding section, the
main-text analysis focuses on DCLM. Figure 5 shows the results for 300M models with L ∈{2, 4, 8} at TPP
40 and 100.
Benefits and costs of loop-specific specialization.
The gains from loop-specific specialization are marginal at
L = 2 but become clear at L ∈{4, 8} (Figure 5). Among the three specialization options, Loop Matrix provides
the largest improvement, though the relative differences are smaller than the gain over the unspecialized
looping baseline. We therefore use Loop Matrix by default in subsequent experiments involving loop-specific
specialization, including those in subsection 3.4. At 300M, these specialization methods increase the number
of non-embedding parameters by only 1.1–4.3%, compared with 51.4% for doubling the model depth. At
comparable training FLOPs, the doubled-depth reference (the brown pentagon) achieves lower BPB than
loop-specific models. Loop-specific specialization therefore offers a parameter-efficient, rather than compute-
efficient, way to improve quality. We observe the same overall trends for 70M–200M models across TPP 10, 40,
and 100, in targeted 500M and 1B experiments, and on the downstream datasets introduced in subsection 3.1.
Full results are provided in Appendix F; a more exhaustive study of specialization methods is left for future
work. Loop-specific specialization improves quality but does not change the fact that the loops must be
evaluated one after another for each generated token.
3.4
Stabilizing PLT and Looping
Looping improves quality but requires loops to run serially during autoregressive decoding. PLT (subsection B.3)
shifts the connections between loops across adjacent tokens, allowing different loops to run in parallel (Figure 6).
In our experiments, however, PLT underperforms Full-Stack Looping (Figure 7). We investigate whether
this quality loss is an inherent limitation of PLT or whether it can be mitigated by reconstructing PLT one
component at a time.
Recovering and improving PLT quality. To isolate the source of the quality degradation, we reconstruct PLT one
component at a time. As defined in Figure 6, CLP passes b(ℓ)

and normalized G-SWA to reconstruct Original PLT (subsection B.3). For the 200M model at both TPP 40
and 80, neither Original PLT nor the intermediate variants formed by adding its components recover the

The current-token embedding matters more than the exact gate. The analysis above uses the fixed gate g(ℓ)
n
= 1
21.
To test whether a more expressive gate improves quality, we compare four alternatives defined in subsection G.3:
fixed 50/50, scalar, rank-8 linear, and rank-8 MLP gates. They perform similarly across model scales, TPP
settings, and numbers of loops, although the rank-8 linear gate is marginally better. We use the rank-8 linear
gate in subsequent experiments. It is shared across loops and adds less than 0.015% to the non-embedding
parameter count. We separately test the role of the current-token embedding in subsection G.4. Removing it
causes shifted PLT to perform worse than Vanilla (No Looping), and several runs diverge. We hypothesize
that, without en, each shifted loop must infer the current token solely from the previous-token state. As this
state represents a contextual prediction rather than the token that actually occurred, prediction errors may
propagate through subsequent loops and accumulate across token positions, causing quality degradation or
divergence. Reintroducing en anchors every loop to the observed current token and limits this accumulation.
Our normalized shifted PLT scales. Hereafter, Normalized Shifted PLT refers to our modified design that uses
the previous token’s hidden state and allows loops to run in parallel, whereas Normalized Unshifted PLT uses
the current token’s hidden state and therefore remains sequential. Both variants separately normalize the
current-token embedding and hidden state, combine them using a rank-8 linear gate, and maintain a separate
KV cache for each loop. Unlike Original PLT, neither uses KV sharing nor G-SWA. We evaluate both variants

Figure 8 Normalized shifted PLT retains the benefit of looping at 1B. DCLM BPB at TPP 10, 20, 40, and 80. Shifted and
unshifted PLT use separate RMS normalization and a rank-8 linear gate. At L = 2, shifted PLT closely tracks the
unshifted control and improves upon Full-Stack Looping; at L = 4, a clearer gap to the unshifted control remains.
Lower BPB is better.
from 100M to 1B. At 1B and L = 2, Normalized Shifted PLT achieves lower DCLM BPB than Full-Stack
Looping at every displayed training budget and closely tracks Normalized Unshifted PLT (Figure 8). At
L = 4, it remains competitive with Full-Stack Looping, although its gap to Normalized Unshifted PLT widens.
The same trends hold across six evaluation corpora at 1B (subsection H.1). Additional sweeps across model
sizes, numbers of loops, and TPP settings appear in subsection H.2 and subsection H.3. We next examine the
training-data and training-FLOP efficiency of looping.
Looping can improve data and FLOP efficiency. As shown in Figure 1, looping can reach a given DCLM BPB
using fewer training tokens than Vanilla (No Looping), suggesting that looping can partly substitute for
additional training data. Data efficiency does not automatically imply FLOP efficiency because each loop
adds computation per token. In the evaluated over-trained regime, however, the reduction in required training
tokens outweighs this additional cost: across model sizes from 100M to 1B, two-loop models trained at TPP
80 reach a given BPB using fewer training FLOPs than Vanilla models trained at TPP 160. This advantage
does not hold uniformly across numbers of loops: although L = 4 can achieve lower absolute BPB, it provides
a weaker improvement per training FLOP (subsection H.4).
4
Related Work
Prior work studies looping from several complementary angles. Scaling studies ask how much additional
looping is worth under different parameter, data, and compute budgets, either through scaling laws or by
comparing shared recurrence with added physical depth (Prairie et al., 2026; Schwethelm et al., 2026). Other
work explores particular choices for where and how recurrence is introduced in the transformer (Cai et al.,
2026; Zeitoun et al., 2026). As these studies use different looping designs and evaluate them under different
resource constraints, it is difficult to separate the effect of the design from the effect of the budget. We instead
vary where and how much to loop within a common dense model family and evaluate these choices under both
parameter and training-compute constraints. Another line of work asks whether every loop should use exactly
the same weights. Relaxed Recursive Transformers (Bae et al., 2025) give each loop a small set of additional
parameters when converting a pretrained transformer into a looped model. We instead train the looped
transformer and its loop-specific adapters together from scratch and test whether these adapters become
more useful as the number of loops increases. Looping also raises a separate challenge during autoregressive
inference. In ordinary looping, the model must finish one loop for a token before starting the next. Parallel
Loop Transformers (PLTs) combat this by shifting information across adjacent token positions so that loop
computations can be parallelized during decoding (Wu et al., 2025). This change alters the information
available to each loop, and recent work reports that gains can diminish as more parallel loops are added (Yang
et al., 2026). This leaves open what the shifted transition must preserve to retain quality as the number of
loops increases. We find that preserving the current-token embedding and separately normalizing it and the
state passed from the previous token substantially improves parallel looping at larger loop counts. Our work
9


therefore connects three aspects of looping within a common setting: where and how much to loop, whether
different loops should use exactly the same weights, and how loops can be parallelized during autoregressive
inference. See Appendix A for a more detailed discussion.
5
Conclusion and Future Directions
We show that looping is a multidimensional design problem that must jointly account for stored parameters,
training FLOPs, data efficiency, and inference efficiency. A promising future direction is to find ways of
combining PLT with speculative decoding to further reduce inference latency. Another is to develop a theory of
how normalization and current-token embedding reinjection affect stability in both the cross-token recurrence
of PLT and the same-token recurrence of standard looping, and to identify which regimes favor each approach.
6
AI Use Statement
In this work, we used generative AI tools, specifically ChatGPT, Codex, Claude, and Claude Code, for
assistance with writing, code development and editing, debugging, and auxiliary sanity checks. We did not
use generative AI tools to develop the core research methodology, formulate the main research hypotheses,
design the experiments, or interpret the experimental results. All AI-generated or AI-suggested text and
code were reviewed by the authors. AI-assisted code was tested and validated for correctness before being
used in the reported experiments. The authors made all methodological and experimental decisions and
independently verified the analyses and reported results. We take full responsibility for the final content of
this work, including any text, code, claims, or artifacts produced with the assistance of generative AI tools.

Limitations
146
14


A
Related Work
Looped Transformers.
Looped Transformers increase effective depth by repeatedly applying shared computa-
tion (Dehghani et al., 2018). Saunshi et al. (2025) show that shared looping can recover much of the benefit of
unique depth on iterative reasoning problems. Geiping et al. (2025) instead study looping as a mechanism for
scaling latent test-time computation, training a language model whose loop depth can vary at inference. More
recently, Zhu et al. (2026) combine looped pretraining with learned depth allocation at substantially larger
data scale. Relatedly, Staircase Attention (Ju et al., 2022) combines looping across sequence time and depth,
with its Ladder variant repeatedly applying a shared Transformer core. Together, these works establish that
repeated computation can provide useful capability and reasoning benefits. Compared with prior looping
work, we provide a more systematic account of the trade-offs among parameter efficiency, training FLOPs,
specialization, and the architectural requirements for efficient autoregressive inference. Our analysis shows
that looping is not uniformly beneficial: the preferred design depends on which resource is constrained.
Scaling and efficiency of weight reuse.
Recent work studies looping from scaling, capacity, and efficiency
perspectives. Prairie et al. (2026) develop a stable looped architecture by analyzing the looped residual
stream as a dynamical system, and use it to derive scaling laws for loop depth and data under fixed parameter
and compute budgets. Schwethelm et al. (2026) instead quantify the effective capacity of repeated shared
computation relative to unique depth, finding that each additional loop provides only partial equivalence to
an additional untied block. Other work modifies the looping architecture itself. Hyperloop combines a looped
middle block with hyper-connections to improve parameter efficiency (Zeitoun et al., 2026), while T2MLR
introduces a temporal pathway through intermediate layers (Cai et al., 2026). Mixture-of-Recursions uses
token-level routing to allocate different loop depths across tokens (Bae et al., 2026). Sparse looped models
use expert routing to allow the same physical layers to realize different effective transformations across loop
applications (Lee et al., 2026). Unlike work that studies a single scaling relation or advocates a particular
architectural modification, we hold the backbone dense and the training setup fixed while jointly varying loop
placement, loop depth, loop-specific specialization, and state propagation during autoregressive decoding. We
evaluate these choices under fixed parameter, training-data, and training-FLOP budgets. This design reveals
trade-offs that are obscured when only one resource is considered. Our goal is therefore not to identify a
universally optimal looping mechanism, but to characterize how the preferred design changes with the resource
constraint.
Relaxing Exact Parameter Sharing.
Strict looping requires the same parameters to serve every depth
application. Relaxed Recursive Transformers (RRTs) relax this constraint by converting a pretrained untied
Transformer into a compact looped model and adding depth-specific low-rank adapters (Bae et al., 2025).
This procedure substantially improves over strict tying and can recover much of the performance lost when
originally distinct layers are collapsed into a shared block. Related work introduces flexibility through
different mechanisms. Hyperloop augments a looped middle block with loop-level hyper-connections, allowing
the residual pathway to vary across loop applications with little additional parameter cost (Zeitoun et al.,
2026). Mixture-of-Recursions instead uses routers to assign individual tokens different loop depths, combining
parameter sharing with adaptive computation (Bae et al., 2026). Sparse looped models select different
experts at different loop applications, so weight sharing need not imply that every loop realizes the same
effective transformation (Lee et al., 2026). Our setting isolates a different question. Rather than converting a
pretrained untied model or introducing routing and expert-specific capacity, we train a dense looped model
from initialization and add small loop-specific corrections to the shared block. We use these corrections
primarily as a diagnostic: does the value of depth-specific capacity increase as the same shared transformation
is applied more times? This controlled comparison distinguishes the general cost of exact sharing from the
benefits of a particular architectural mechanism, while preserving a common backbone, training setup, and
parameter-sharing structure.
Parallel Looping and State Propagation.
Conventional looping reduces the number of unique parameters,
but places successive loop applications on the sequential path of autoregressive generation. Parallel Loop
Transformers (PLTs) reorganize this dependency through Cross-Loop Parallelism (CLP): recurrent state is
shifted across token positions, allowing loop stages to be pipelined during decoding. PLTs combine this
15


schedule with shared KV caches and gated sliding-window attention to bound cache growth (Wu et al.,
2025). LoopCoder-v2 studies this design at larger scale and finds a strongly non-monotonic dependence
on loop count: a second loop improves performance, whereas three or more loops regress as the benefit
of further refinement is outweighed by the positional mismatch introduced by CLP (Yang et al., 2026).
Related work studies how looped dynamics can remain stable and input-dependent. Labovich (2026) analyze
fixed-point stability in looped Transformers, showing that recall together with outer normalization can yield
reachable, input-dependent, and trainable dynamics. Kim et al. (2026) develop source-centered state evolution,
which preserves an input-conditioned anchor while allowing deviations from that anchor to drive recurrent
computation. Our work addresses the interface created specifically by cross-token parallelization. Under CLP,
a loop stage must combine a representation of the current token with a shifted state produced at a different
token position and loop depth. We find that the main limitation of naïve PLT is this shifted re-injection
transition, rather than cache sharing itself. Our controlled ablations isolate the contributions of the cross-token
shift, attention and cache mechanisms, normalization, recurrent-state mixing, and access to the current-token
representation. This identifies separate normalization of the current-token and shifted-state streams, together
with preservation of the current-token path, as the central repair. Thus, rather than proposing normalization
or input preservation as general primitives, we characterize their role in making the parallel looping interface
effective.
16


B
Background
Notation.
We use bold uppercase letters to denote matrices (e.g., X, W ), bold lowercase letters to denote
vectors (e.g., x), and unbolded letters for scalars and indices.
Calligraphic letters denote functions or
transformations (e.g., F), while Greek letters are used for scalar- or vector-valued parameters when convenient.
For a positive integer K, we write [K] := {1, . . . , K}. We use [A, B] to denote concatenation along the feature
dimension and [A; B] to denote stacking along the sequence or row dimension. The operator ⊙denotes
element-wise multiplication, and σ(·) denotes the logistic sigmoid. For a Transformer with L physical layers, we
denote the parameters of layer m ∈[L] by θm, and write Θ := {θ1, . . . , θL} for the collection of all Transformer-
layer parameters. When a layer or block is reused across multiple loop iterations, the same parameter set θm
is used at every corresponding invocation unless explicitly stated otherwise. Architecture-specific notation is
introduced alongside the corresponding construction.
B.1
Looped Transformers
Transformer blocks.
We begin with the basic unit of computation that will be reused throughout the paper.
Let AθA denote causal self-attention and MθM denote the position-wise MLP. We use a pre-normalized
Transformer block, in which attention and the MLP each act on a normalized residual stream.
Definition 3:
Pre-normalized Transformer block. Given a sequence representation X ∈RN×D, a
Transformer block with parameters θ = (θA, θM) computes
U := X + AθA(LNA(X)) ,
Tθ(X) := U + MθM (LNM(U)) .
A standard depth-B Transformer composes B such blocks, each with its own parameters (Note that H
denotes hidden representations):
H0 := X,
Hb := Tθb(Hb−1) ,
∀b ∈[B].
In a standard Transformer, computational depth and parameter depth grow together: moving from Hb−1 to
Hb introduces both computation by another transformer block and a new parameter set θb. Increasing depth
therefore increases not only the amount of computation performed on each token, but also the number of
parameters that must be stored. Looping breaks this coupling. Rather than introducing a new transformation
at every additional depth, it repeatedly applies the same transformation, allowing computational depth to
grow while keeping the parameters fixed.
Weight reuse through looping.
To formalize this distinction, let Fθ denote any Transformer computation
chosen for reuse. Depending on the construction, Fθ may correspond to a single Transformer block, a contiguous
group of blocks, or the entire Transformer stack. A looped Transformer applies this same parameterized
function repeatedly.
Definition 4:
Looped Transformer (Saunshi et al., 2025; Geiping et al., 2025; Dehghani et al., 2018).
Given an input representation X, the L-loop model induced by Fθ is
H(0) := X,
H(ℓ) := Fθ

H(ℓ−1)
,
∀ℓ∈[L].
The parameter set θ is shared across all loop iterations. Thus, the superscript (ℓ) indexes repeated
applications of the same transformation rather than distinct parameterized layers.
17


This separation between stored depth and executed depth is the central appeal of looping.
Suppose a
single application of Fθ contains P parameters and incurs compute cost C. Repeating it L times requires
approximately LC compute while still storing only the same P parameters: Ploop ≈P, Cloop ≈LC. Looping
therefore trades additional computation for parameter reuse: it can increase the effective computational depth
of a model without requiring a proportional increase in parameter memory. The extra depth is not free,
however, since the reused function must still be evaluated at every loop iteration. This definition deliberately
leaves Fθ unspecified. In a Transformer, there are several natural choices for what computation to reuse and
in what order to reuse it. One may repeat the entire stack, repeatedly apply each layer before advancing
to the next, restrict reuse to a middle portion of the network, or loop only particular subcomponents such
as attention or the MLP. These choices induce different computational graphs despite sharing the same
underlying principle of parameter reuse. We define the constructions considered in this work next.
Loop placement and computation order.
A natural next question is where this weight reuse can occur and in
what order the repeated operations are executed. These choices are distinct: two architectures may reuse
exactly the same set of Transformer blocks the same number of times, yet induce different computational
graphs because the repetitions are ordered differently. To make this distinction explicit, we write G◦R for R
successive applications of a transformation G.
Definition 5:
Full-Stack and Per-Layer Looping. Let Fθ = TθB ◦· · · ◦Tθ1 denote a stack of B Transformer
blocks.
1. Full-Stack Looping repeatedly applies the complete stack of the layers of a transformer model:
H(L) = F◦L
θ (X).
2. Per-Layer Looping instead completes the looping of each block before advancing to the next. If
block b is applied Rb times, define
Z1,0 := X,
Zb,0 := Zb−1,Rb−1,
∀b ∈{2, . . . , B},
Zb,r := Tθb(Zb,r−1) ,
∀b ∈[B], ∀r ∈[Rb].
When Rb = L for every block, Full-Stack and Per-Layer Looping use the same stored parameters and perform
the same number of Transformer-block evaluations; they differ only in the ordering of those evaluations.
Full-Stack Looping repeatedly traverses the full stack of transformer blocks, whereas Per-Layer Looping
completes all loops of a block before advancing to the next. Since a Transformer block is itself composed of
attention and MLP sublayers, we next consider whether reuse should operate on the entire block or on its
individual components.
Independent attention and MLP looping.
Per-Layer Looping can be refined further by choosing which
subcomponents of a Transformer block are reused. To make these choices explicit, we define again the residual
attention and MLP maps as follows.

c
MθM (X) := X + MθM (LNM(X)) ,
Then a standard Transformer block can be written as
Tθ = c

AθA.
This decomposition lets us distinguish whether repeated computation is allocated to attention, to the MLP,
or to the complete block.

In every case, repeated applications of a sublayer reuse the same parameters.
These designs separate two aspects of looping. Joint looping repeatedly alternates token mixing and channel-
wise transformation, whereas independent looping groups repeated applications of each operation. Attention-
only and MLP-only looping further isolate whether additional computation is more useful for token mixing or
for position-wise transformation. The constructions above determine what computation is reused and how
repeated operations are ordered. A separate design choice is where in the network this reuse is introduced.
In particular, recent looped architectures often preserve distinct input and output layers while restricting
recurrence to an intermediate portion of the network (Prairie et al., 2026). We introduce this middle-looping
construction next.
Middle-Stack Looping.
The constructions above specify what is repeated and how repeated computations are
ordered. A separate choice is where recurrence is introduced. Instead of reusing every block in the network,
one can preserve unique transformations at the beginning and end of the model and restrict recurrence to a
contiguous middle region. This retains input and output processing while concentrating repeated computation
in the interior of the network. Within the looping region, the same ordering choices remain: the selected
blocks can either be repeated individually or reused together as a complete stack.
Definition 7:
Middle-Layer and Middle-Stack Looping. Consider a depth-B Transformer and choose
indices 1 ≤s ≤e ≤B. We partition the model into a prefix, a recurrent middle region, and a suffix:
Fpre := Tθs−1 ◦· · · ◦Tθ1,
Fmid := Tθe ◦· · · ◦Tθs,
Fpost := TθB ◦· · · ◦Tθe+1,
where an empty prefix or suffix is interpreted as the identity map.
1. Middle-Layer Looping repeats each block in the selected middle region before advancing to the
next block. If block b is applied Rb times, then
Zs,0 := Fpre(X),
Zb,0 := Zb−1,Rb−1,
∀b ∈{s + 1, . . . , e},
Zb,r := Tθb(Zb,r−1) ,
∀b ∈{s, . . . , e}, ∀r ∈[Rb],
Fmiddle-layer(X) := Fpost(Ze,Re) .
2. Middle-Stack Looping treats the entire selected middle region as a single composite transformation
19


and repeats that transformation L times:
Z(0) := Fpre(X),
Z(ℓ) := Fmid

Z(ℓ−1)
,
∀ℓ∈[L],
Fmiddle-stack(X) := Fpost

Z(L)
.
Middle-layer and Middle-Stack Looping therefore reuse the same region of the network but impose different
computation orders. When every selected block is repeated the same number of times, Rb = L, Middle-Layer
Looping applies all repetitions of one block before moving to the next, T ◦L
θe ◦· · · ◦T ◦L
θs
whereas Middle-Stack
Looping repeatedly traverses the entire recurrent region, (Tθe ◦· · · ◦Tθs)◦L . In both cases, the prefix and suffix
are evaluated only once and retain their own parameters. Middle looping therefore separates the question of
where additional computation is allocated from the question of how that computation is ordered.
Why the form of looping matters.
Looping is often described simply as a way to obtain more computation
from a fixed set of parameters. This description is correct, but incomplete. In practice, the usefulness of weight
reuse depends on which resource is scarce. Parameters, training FLOPs, memory movement, communication,
and sequential inference time impose different constraints, and different looping constructions trade among
these resources in different ways. Consequently, two architectures that reuse the same number of parameters
can behave very differently as computational systems. The choice of what to loop, where to loop it, and in
what order to execute the repeated computation is therefore not merely an architectural detail; it determines
what kind of resource efficiency the model is attempting to obtain.
Consider first the distinction between full-stack and middle looping. Full-Stack Looping repeatedly evaluates
the entire Transformer, and therefore uses the additional compute budget everywhere in the network. If
parameter memory is the primary constraint while additional FLOPs are comparatively cheap, this is a
natural strategy: every extra loop provides another complete pass through attention, MLP computation,
and the hierarchy of learned representations. In such a regime, there is little reason to leave part of the
network idle simply to save computation. The situation changes when training FLOPs themselves become the
limiting resource. Repeating the complete network may then spend substantial compute on layers for which
additional evaluation provides relatively little marginal benefit. Middle looping offers a different trade-off:
the beginning and end of the network are evaluated once, while the additional compute is concentrated in a
smaller recurrent core. Thus, even when full-stack and middle looping are motivated by the same goal of
parameter reuse, they answer different resource-allocation questions. The former asks how much quality can
be extracted from a fixed parameter budget when more compute is available, while the latter asks whether
that additional compute can be spent more selectively.
Computation order introduces a different systems consideration. Suppose the same collection of layers is
evaluated the same number of times. Stack looping cycles through the entire collection before returning
to the first layer, whereas Per-Layer Looping repeatedly evaluates one layer before moving on to the next.
Their arithmetic FLOPs can therefore be identical, yet their execution patterns are different. This distinction
can matter in regimes where the cost of moving parameters is comparable to, or larger than, the cost of
performing arithmetic with parameters that are already resident on the accelerator. A per-layer schedule
repeatedly uses the same weights in consecutive operations, potentially allowing more computation to be
performed while those weights remain locally available. Stack looping instead revisits a layer only after the
intervening layers have been executed. We do not assume that one design is universally faster; the answer
depends on the hardware, batch size, and whether execution is compute- or memory-bound, but the two
designs expose different opportunities for parameter reuse at the systems level even when their nominal
FLOP counts are matched. The distinction between attention and MLP looping is similarly motivated by
the fact that Transformer components need not have comparable costs. In a standard dense Transformer,
repeating attention spends additional computation on token-to-token interaction, whereas repeating the MLP
spends it on position-wise feature transformation. In other model families, however, the systems consequences
can be much larger. Consider a mixture-of-experts (MoE) Transformer in which the MLP is replaced by
routed experts distributed across different accelerators or nodes. Repeating the MLP may then require
20


not only additional matrix multiplications, but also repeated token routing, all-to-all communication, and
expert-weight access. Attention-only looping would increase effective computational depth without repeatedly
incurring those expert-related costs. Conversely, for very long sequences, attention itself can become an
increasingly expensive part of the model because its compute and memory traffic depend on sequence length,
making additional MLP computation comparatively attractive. These examples are not claims about our
dense experiments as we do not evaluate MoE models here, but they illustrate why deciding whether to
loop attention, the MLP, or the complete block can become an important systems choice rather than a
cosmetic architectural ablation. Finally, reuse depth determines how aggressively the model makes this
parameter–compute trade. Increasing the number of loops allows the same parameters to support progressively
greater effective depth, which is the central appeal of looping. At the same time, every additional application
must still be executed. Training FLOPs therefore increase with loop count, and under conventional looping the
loops are also executed sequentially, leading to slower autoregressive generation during inference. A model can
consequently become more parameter-efficient while becoming less attractive from the standpoint of training
compute or autoregressive latency. This tension becomes particularly important at large loop factors: the
question is not simply whether more loops continue to improve loss, but whether their marginal improvement
justifies the additional computation and sequential depth.
Taken together, these examples show why there is no single notion of the “best” looping architecture independent
of the resource regime. Full-stack looping may be preferable when parameters are expensive and additional
FLOPs are available; middle looping may be preferable when compute must be allocated more selectively;
Per-Layer Looping changes the pattern with which shared weights are reused by the hardware; and attention-
versus MLP-level looping can become important when the two components have different computational or
communication costs. The architectural choices introduced above therefore correspond to different ways of
allocating a fixed parameter budget across computation, memory movement, communication, and sequential
depth.
Our architectural ablations.
Motivated by these trade-offs, we systematically vary loop placement, com-
putation order, sublayer choice, and reuse depth across model scales and training budgets. We compare
these constructions both at matched data and as a function of executed FLOPs, since the two views answer
different questions: the former measures how much quality can be extracted from a fixed parameter budget
when additional computation is available, while the latter measures how effectively each construction uses
a constrained compute budget. Individual points in this design space have precedents. Prior work repeats
complete Transformer stacks (Saunshi et al., 2025), while other approaches retain distinct outer layers and
apply looping to a middle stack (Zeitoun et al., 2026; Prairie et al., 2026). We therefore do not treat any
individual looping primitive as the contribution of this work. Rather, our goal is to compare these choices
within a common dense, from-scratch setting and determine how their relative behavior changes with model
scale, training budget, loop depth, and the resource under which they are compared.
Sequential inference.
The discussion above concerns how much repeated computation should be performed
and where that computation should be allocated. It leaves open a separate issue that becomes especially
important at inference time: in conventional looping, the additional effective depth is realized through a
sequence of dependent computations. Since H(ℓ) = Fθ
 H(ℓ−1)
, loop ℓcannot be evaluated before loop ℓ−1
has completed. Increasing the loop factor therefore increases not only executed computation, but also the
amount of sequential computation on the inference critical path. This creates a tension that is not captured by
training FLOPs alone. A looping construction may be attractive because it achieves better quality for a fixed
parameter budget, or even because it provides a favorable quality–FLOP trade-off, yet still be unattractive
for autoregressive decoding if obtaining that gain requires proportionally more sequential model evaluations
for every generated token. In other words, reducing the amount of repeated computation, for example by
looping only a middle region, does not resolve the fact that the remaining loop iterations are still ordered
sequentially. Parameter efficiency, compute efficiency, and inference latency are therefore distinct objectives.
This observation motivates the next question we study: whether the benefits of repeated computation can be
retained while relaxing the serial dependency imposed by conventional looping.
21


B.2
Low-Rank Adaptation and Relaxed Weight Sharing
A looped Transformer repeats the same layer, or group of layers, L times. Normally, every loop uses the same
attention and MLP weights. Sharing the weights in this way keeps the model small, since adding loops does
not require storing new Transformer layers. However, the loops do not all receive the same kind of input.
The first loop processes the initial representation, while later loops process representations that have already
passed through the shared layers one or more times. It may therefore be useful for different loops to learn
slightly different transformations.
One option would be to give every loop its own attention and MLP weights. This would provide complete
specialization, but it would also make the loops equivalent to separately parameterized Transformer layers.
The number of stored parameters would then grow with the number of loops, removing much of the motivation
for weight reuse. We instead use Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) (Hu et al., 2021) to place a small loop-specific
correction around a larger shared weight matrix.
Definition 8:
Loop-specific LoRA. Consider a weight matrix W ∈Rdout×din that is shared across L
loops. In loop ℓ∈{1, . . . , L}, we use
W (ℓ) = W + α
r B(ℓ)A(ℓ)
, where A(ℓ) ∈Rr×din and B(ℓ) ∈Rdout×r are learned separately for that loop. The rank r controls
the size of the correction, and α/r controls its scale. Each loop-specific correction adds r(din + dout)
parameters, rather than the dindout parameters required to store another complete version of W .
For one adapted weight matrix, using the same weights in every loop requires dindout parameters. Loop-specific
LoRA requires dindout + Lr(din + dout) parameters, while giving every loop a different weight matrix requires
Ldindout parameters. Loop-specific LoRA therefore lies between these two choices: most parameters remain
shared, but each loop has a small amount of capacity with which to specialize.
Prior work explores a similar middle ground in Relaxed Recursive Transformers (RRTs) (Bae et al., 2025).
RRT begins with a pretrained Transformer whose layers have different weights and converts it into a smaller
looped model by replacing groups of those layers with repeated uses of shared weights. Because this conversion
removes differences among the original layers, RRT gives each loop a low-rank adapter initialized from a
truncated SVD of the difference between the original weight and its shared replacement. The converted model
is then trained further. Our setting differs because we do not begin with a larger pretrained model or attempt
to preserve its layer-specific weights. Instead, the Transformer is looped from the beginning, and the shared
weights and loop-specific adapters are trained jointly from initialization. We use this setting to ask whether
loop-specific specialization is useful during looped pretraining itself and whether its value increases as the
same weights are reused across more loops.
B.3
Parallel Loop Transformers
In conventional looping, loop ℓfor token n receives the output of loop ℓ−1 for that same token. The model
must therefore finish loop 1 before starting loop 2, finish loop 2 before starting loop 3, and continue in this
way through all L loops. Producing one token consequently requires L consecutive evaluations of the shared
Transformer. Parallel Loop Transformers (PLTs) (Wu et al., 2025) remove this dependency. For loop ℓ, PLT
combines the current token representation with the output of loop ℓ−1 from the preceding token n −1. When
decoding token n, the model has already processed token n −1, so all of these preceding-token outputs are
available. The inputs to the L loops can therefore be constructed at the same time and evaluated together,
rather than waiting for one loop to finish before beginning the next. PLT still performs L evaluations and
therefore does not reduce total FLOPs, but parallel hardware can execute them concurrently, reducing the
time required to produce each token. Formally, let t = (t1, . . . , tN) denote a token sequence. At position
n ∈[N], let en ∈RD denote the token embedding, b(ℓ)
n
∈RD the representation supplied to loop ℓ, and
h(ℓ)
n
∈RD the corresponding output state. We use the boundary condition h(ℓ)
0
= 0 and initialize the first
loop with the token embedding, b(1)
n
= en. The key departure from conventional looping appears in every
22


subsequent loop.

loop combines the current token embedding with the previous token’s state from the preceding loop:

Here, F(ℓ)

from conventional looping is the recurrent source: PLT uses h(ℓ−1)

The goal of PLT is not to reduce the total computation introduced by looping. Training still evaluates the
loops sequentially, and inference still applies the shared Transformer L times. Instead, PLT targets the way
this computation is executed during autoregressive decoding. Conventional looping requires L consecutive
evaluations for every generated token. These single-token evaluations expose little parallel work and repeatedly
access the same shared weights. PLT reorganizes the dependencies so that the L loop inputs become available
together and can be processed as a batch. This can reduce decoding latency and increase arithmetic intensity
by performing more computation per weight load. We first describe the training algorithm below (as seen in
Wu et al. (2025)).
PLT Training.
Let E := [e1; . . . ; eN], B(ℓ) := [b(ℓ)
1 ; . . . ; b(ℓ)
N ], and H(ℓ) := [h(ℓ)
1 ; . . . ; h(ℓ)
N ]. We define the one-
position causal shift by Shift([h1; . . . ; hN]) := [0; h1; . . . ; hN−1]. The first loop receives the token embeddings
directly, while every subsequent loop receives the embeddings together with the shifted state of the preceding
loop:
B(1) := E,
B(ℓ) := E + Shift

structure in an important way. Conventional looping connects successive loop iterations at the same token
position, so the next loop cannot begin until the preceding loop for that token has completed. PLT instead
makes the input to loop ℓat the current position depend on a state that was produced at the preceding
position. During training, the full sequence is available, so the model is evaluated one loop at a time while
retaining parallelism over token positions. Below we describe the training algorithm for PLT.

On the other hand, the cost of looping appears differently during training and inference. During training,
the entire token sequence is available, so all token positions can be processed in parallel within each loop.
The L loops must still be evaluated one after another, and we accept this additional training cost. During
autoregressive decoding, however, tokens are generated one at a time. If the L loops are also evaluated
consecutively, every additional loop directly increases the time required to generate each token.
CLP is designed to improve this inference-time behavior rather than reduce training compute. Before token n
is processed, token n −1 has already produced and cached the states {h(ℓ)
n−1}L−1
ℓ=1 . The L inputs for token
n can therefore be constructed together, stacked along the loop dimension, and passed through the shared
Transformer as a single batch. The model performs the same L loop computations, but parallel hardware can
execute them together rather than consecutively. Because every loop uses the same Transformer weights, this
batching can also improve arithmetic intensity by applying each loaded weight to multiple loop inputs. Note
that, at the beginning of a sequence, some preceding-token states are unavailable and are replaced by the zero
boundary state. After the prompt or initial tokens populate the cache, decoding reaches a steady state in
which every loop receives a state computed at the preceding token position. The algorithm below describes
one decoding step in this steady-state regime.

Here, Batch(·) stacks the L loop inputs along an independent batch dimension rather than concatenating
them along the token dimension. The shared Transformer Fθ processes this batch in one invocation, and
[Hn]ℓdenotes the output corresponding to loop ℓ. The algorithm therefore maps the loop dimension onto the
batch dimension during steady-state decoding.
Shared global and loop-local context.
Evaluating the L loops together reduces the delay caused by running
them one after another, but it does not reduce the memory needed for attention. If each loop stores its own
sequence-wide KV cache, then a model with sequence length N, hidden dimension D, and L loops requires
O(LND) cache memory. PLT avoids this cost by storing one global cache, produced by the first loop, and
sharing it with all later loops. This gives every loop access to the full preceding sequence without storing
another complete set of keys and values.
Using only the shared cache, however, would prevent later loops from retaining attention states produced by
their own computations. PLT therefore gives each later loop an additional private cache containing only its
most recent w positions. The shared cache provides global context from the first loop, while the private window
provides recent context specific to the current loop. Together, these caches require O(ND + (L −1)wD)
memory.
PLT combines the two sources of context using gated sliding-window attention (G-SWA). For loop ℓ≥2, let
Q(ℓ) denote its queries, (Kshare, Vshare) the global KV cache from the first loop, and (K(ℓ)
local, V (ℓ)
local) its private
KV cache over the most recent w positions. G-SWA computes attention over the shared and local caches
24


separately and uses a learned head-wise gate to combine the two outputs. Below we describe this concretely.
Definition 10:
Gated Sliding Window Attention (G-SWA). For every loop ℓ≥2, define
Y (ℓ)
global := Attn

Q(ℓ), Kshare, Vshare


Y (ℓ)
local := SWA

Q(ℓ), K(ℓ)
local, V (ℓ)




fgate


,


1 −g(ℓ)

Here, fgate produces one scalar gate for each attention head, which is broadcast across the corresponding
head representation. Each head can therefore determine how much of its output should come from
loop-local recent context versus the global context shared from the first loop.
The shared first-loop cache requires O(ND) memory, while the private sliding-window caches contribute
O((L −1)wD). The resulting KV-cache memory complexity is therefore O(ND + (L −1)wD) rather than
O(LND). When w ≪N, G-SWA allows later loops to retain their own recent attention state without
requiring a separate sequence-length cache for every recurrent stage. The two main components of PLT
therefore address different costs of recurrent computation. The cross-loop transition in Def. 9 changes the
dependency structure, allowing recurrent stages to be pipelined across token positions during autoregressive
decoding. KV sharing and G-SWA address the separate memory cost of maintaining attention state for those
stages. The former supplies a common global context, while the latter restores a small amount of loop-specific
context that would otherwise disappear under complete KV sharing.
Relation to our study.
Our study examines whether the change in information flow introduced by CLP affects
model quality independently of its inference benefits. In the original PLT transition, each later loop directly
adds the current-token embedding en to the shifted state h(ℓ−1)
n−1 . These representations play different roles
and may have different scales, so how they are combined can determine whether parallel looping preserves the
benefits of ordinary looping. We isolate this effect by reconstructing PLT component by component, separating
the shifted transition from KV sharing and G-SWA. We then vary access to the current-token representation,
normalization, and the mechanism used to combine it with the shifted state. These experiments distinguish
limitations of CLP itself from limitations of the transition and cache mechanisms used to implement it.
25


C
Model Sizes and Training Budgets
We train six model sizes, ranging from 70M to 1B, and vary the amount of training data from 10 to 160
tokens per parameter (TPP). This section specifies the architecture used at each model size, explains how the
model-size labels are assigned, and describes how TPP determines the training-token budget.
Table 1 Model configurations used in our experiments. Transformer layers give the number of Transformer blocks. Model
width is the dimension of the representation passed between layers, and MLP width is the intermediate dimension of
the SwiGLU MLP. Q/KV gives the numbers of query heads and key–value heads, respectively. The first parameter
column excludes the input embedding and is used to assign the model label and set the training-token budget. The
final column includes the input embedding and gives the total number of trainable parameters. Parameter counts are
reported in millions. All models use a vocabulary of 128,256 tokens, a context length of 8,192, and an attention-head
dimension of 128.

Model-size labels.
We assign each model the label closest to its number of parameters excluding the input
embedding, rounding up or down to the nearest scale in our model ladder. For example, the model with
489.554M such parameters is labeled 500M, while the model with 967.311M is labeled 1B. These labels
are therefore convenient rounded names rather than exact parameter counts. The total parameter count is
larger because it also includes the input embedding. This difference is largest for the smaller models. For
example, the model labeled 70M has 77.726M parameters when the input embedding is excluded and 143.397M
parameters when it is included. We retain the same model-size label for looped variants so that it continues
to identify the underlying model dimensions, even when weight sharing changes the number of parameters
that must be stored.
Training-token budgets.
TPP specifies the number of training tokens allocated per parameter, excluding
the input embedding. We use TPP values of 10, 20, 40, 80, and 160. The target number of training tokens
is D = TPP × Pnon-embed, where Pnon-embed is the parameter count in the second-to-last column of Table 1.
For example, the 100M model has 135.070M non-embedding parameters and is trained on approximately
5.403 billion tokens at TPP 40. Across all model sizes and TPP values, the training budgets range from 0.777
billion to 154.770 billion tokens. For looped models, parameters reused across multiple loops are counted once,
and any loop-specific parameters are also included. Adding loops also increases training FLOPs even when it
does not increase the parameter count or token budget. We therefore report training tokens and training
FLOPs separately when comparing models. We note that in Table 1, the non-embedding parameter count
used to determine the token budget omits the RMSNorm parameters. These account for less than 0.008% of
every model and do not meaningfully change either the reported model size or its token budget.
Models, training, data, and evaluation.
We train models with 70M, 100M, 200M, 300M, 500M, and 1B
parameters, containing 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, and 16 stored transformer layers, respectively. All models use grouped-
query attention, SwiGLU MLPs, RoPE with θ = 10,000, document-causal attention, a context length of 8,192,
and an attention head dimension of 128. We use a Llama-3-compatible tiktoken tokenizer with a vocabulary
of 128,256 and untied input and output embeddings. We train in bfloat16 with AdamW (β1 = 0.9, β2 = 0.95,
ϵ = 10−8), gradient clipping at 1.0, and a peak learning rate of 3 × 10−4. The learning rate is warmed up over
the first 10% of training and then cosine-decayed. Training budgets range from 10 to 160 tokens per parameter
(TPP), and all models are trained with the same random seeds. Additional model configurations and training
26


budgets are given in Appendix C. All models use the same pretraining mixture of natural language, code,
mathematical, and scientific content, with the data sources, sampling distribution, preprocessing, and tokenizer
held fixed across architectures. We use a fixed subset of the DCLM Baseline 1.0 validation set (Li et al.,
2024), comprising approximately 41 million scored tokens, as our primary language-modeling evaluation
corpus. We use DCLM as our primary metric because it is the largest corpus in our evaluation suite and
provides the broadest estimate of language-modeling quality. To test whether the observed trends generalize
across domains, we additionally evaluate on the Paloma benchmark (Magnusson et al., 2024) versions of C4,
WikiText-103, Dolma v1.5, RedPajama, and Dolma Programming Languages (Raffel et al., 2020; Merity
et al., 2016; Soldaini et al., 2024; Weber et al., 2024). We also evaluate likelihood on the mathematical and
code datasets GSM8K (Cobbe et al., 2021), MATH (Hendrycks et al., 2021b), HumanEval (Chen et al.,
2021), and MBPP (Austin et al., 2021). For these evaluations, we teacher-force the reference sequence and
report bits per byte (BPB; lower is better). We separately evaluate downstream multiple-choice performance
on ARC-Easy and ARC-Challenge (Clark et al., 2018), HellaSwag (Zellers et al., 2019), PIQA (Bisk et al.,
2019), WinoGrande (Sakaguchi et al., 2019), OpenBookQA (Mihaylov et al., 2018), CommonsenseQA (Talmor
et al., 2019), and SocialIQA (Sap et al., 2019). For each question, we score every candidate answer using its
average negative log-likelihood per tokenizer token and predict the candidate with the lowest score. We report
NLL-ranked accuracy as well as the normalized NLL assigned to the correct answer. The headline downstream
result is the unweighted mean of NLL-ranked accuracy across these eight tasks. We report MMLU (Hendrycks
et al., 2021a) and MMLU-Pro (Wang et al., 2024) separately as knowledge diagnostics. These multiple-choice
evaluations measure discrimination among the provided answer choices rather than freely generated answers.
27

The headline figure combines two complementary evaluations of looping. The relative-efficiency panels measure
how much training data or total training compute a candidate model requires to attain a given DCLM BPB,
relative to a fitted Vanilla (no looping) baseline. Here, we provide the raw scaling curves, fitting procedure,
and task-level results underlying these comparisons.

Figure 9 Raw scaling curves underlying the relative-efficiency analysis. We plot observed DCLM validation BPB together
with unconstrained Vanilla scaling-law fits. Left: BPB as a function of actual training tokens for fixed 1B models
across training-token budgets. Middle: BPB as a function of total training FLOPs across model families from 100M to
1B. Vanilla models use TPP = 160, whereas looping models use two loops and TPP = 80. Points show observed runs,
connecting lines show empirical trajectories, and dashed black curves show fitted Vanilla scaling laws. Lower BPB is
better.
Looping increases the computation applied to each token but may reduce the amount of training needed to
reach a given quality. We measure data efficiency by the number of training tokens required by a model
to reach a target BPB, and FLOP efficiency by the total training FLOPs required by a model to reach
the same BPB. Following Team (2026), we fit two curves to the BPB achieved by Vanilla (No Looping)
models: one as a function of training tokens and one as a function of total training FLOPs. Both use
L(x) = Ax−α + E, with A, α, and E estimated by unweighted, unconstrained nonlinear least squares
minimizing P
i[Li −(Ax−α
i
+ E)]2. Only non-looping models are used to fit these curves. For a looped model
achieving BPB L′ at cost x′, we estimate the cost that a non-looping model would require to achieve the
same BPB as f −1(L′) = [A/(L′ −E)]1/α. We define the relative efficiency gain as EGx = f −1(L′)/x′, where
EGx > 1 means that the looped model uses fewer tokens or FLOPs than the fitted non-looping baseline.
For data efficiency, we fit 1B non-looping models across training-token budgets. The looping models reach
a given BPB using fewer tokens, with the modified PLT variants providing the largest relative gains. For
FLOP efficiency, we fit non-looping models from 100M to 1B using their total training FLOPs. In the
evaluated over-trained regime, two-loop models trained at TPP 80 reach a given BPB using fewer FLOPs than
non-looping models trained at TPP 160. The key conclusion is that looping is not only parameter-efficient. In
the evaluated over-trained regime, its additional computation per token reduces the required training data
enough to lower total training FLOPs, making looping both data- and FLOP-efficient. This benefit is not
uniform, however, and weakens as the number of loops increases.
D.2
Task-Level Results Behind the Headline Comparison
The right panel of Figure 1 reports the unweighted mean across eight reasoning and question-answering tasks.
Here, we provide task-level results and report MMLU and MMLU-Pro separately to evaluate performance
across a broad range of academic subjects. We compare four 1B model families at TPP ∈{10, 20, 40, 80, 160}:
Vanilla (L = 1), Full-Stack Looping (L = 2, labeled Standard Looping in the headline figure), Normalized
Shifted PLT (L = 2), and Normalized Unshifted PLT (L = 2). Both PLT variants use the rank-8 linear gate.

PIQA, WinoGrande, OpenBookQA, CommonsenseQA, and Social IQA. For each question, the model predicts the

Downstream metrics.
For each question, we score every answer option c by its average negative log-likelihood
(NLL) per tokenizer token, qc = −1
Tc
PTc
t=1 log pθ(xc,t | prompt, xc,<t), where Tc is the number of tokens
in option c, xc,t is its t-th token, and xc,<t denotes its preceding tokens.
We predict the option with
the lowest qc. Figure 10 reports NLL-ranked accuracy on ARC Easy, ARC Challenge, HellaSwag, PIQA,
WinoGrande, OpenBookQA, CommonsenseQA, and Social IQA. The headline figure reports the unweighted
mean across these eight tasks. The upper panel of Figure 12 reports macro-averaged accuracy for MMLU
and MMLU-Pro. NLL-ranked accuracy records only whether the correct answer option has the best score,
not how strongly it is preferred. For a question with C answer options, we therefore normalize the scores as
π(c) = exp(−qc)/ PC
j=1 exp(−qj), and report the target NLL −log π(c⋆), where c⋆is the correct option. A
lower value means that the model assigns greater relative preference to the correct option, even when the
predicted answer remains unchanged. These results appear in Figure 11 and the lower panel of Figure 12.
Dashed lines show the random-choice baselines 1/C for accuracy and log C for target NLL. Both metrics
evaluate the provided answer options rather than free-form generation.
Looping improves performance on most downstream tasks.
At TPP = 160, Full-Stack Looping, Normalized
Shifted PLT, and Normalized Unshifted PLT outperform Vanilla on six of the eight tasks in Figure 10. The
clearest improvements occur on ARC Challenge and HellaSwag, with additional gains on ARC Easy, PIQA,
WinoGrande, and OpenBookQA. CommonsenseQA remains close to the random-choice baseline and varies
non-monotonically across training budgets, while Social IQA shows no consistent improvement. No looping
method dominates every task, but Normalized Shifted PLT generally tracks the sequential looping methods,
suggesting that parallel loop execution does not impose a systematic downstream-accuracy penalty.
Looping lowers correct-answer NLL on most downstream tasks.
The target-NLL results in Figure 11 show the
same general pattern as accuracy. At TPP = 160, every looping method achieves lower choice-normalized target
NLL than Vanilla on the same six tasks. CommonsenseQA remains variable and close to its random-choice
baseline, while Social IQA shows smaller, method-dependent differences. Thus, the aggregate improvement is
distributed across several tasks rather than driven by a single benchmark, although it is not universal.
