E
Additional Results on Looping Designs and Regimes
This appendix section provides the additional results supporting subsection 3.2. The 100M and 500M results
shown in Figure 3 are complemented by experiments at 70M, 200M, and 300M, a separate 1B study, and
evaluations on additional datasets. We first summarize the models, loop factors, and evaluation setup used in
these experiments, and then present the full DCLM results, other dataset comparisons, the 1B extension, and
mathematical and code likelihood evaluations.
E.1
Experimental Setup for the Additional Results
Models and loop factors.
For 70M and 100M models, we evaluate TPP values 10, 20, 40, 70, 100, 120, 140,
and 160, with loop factors L ∈{1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32}. For 200M, 300M, and 500M models, we evaluate TPP values
10, 20, 40, 70, and 100, with loop factors L ∈{1, 2, 4, 8}. Not every configuration produces a stable run. The
1B experiments use a smaller set of loop factors and are described separately in subsection E.4.
Looping designs and training compute.
We use the same looping designs introduced in subsection B.1 and
illustrated in Figure 2. In the figure legends, Stack Loop denotes Full-Stack Looping, Layer Loop denotes
per-layer looping, and Both Loop denotes separately looping the attention and MLP sublayers. All designs
use exact parameter sharing across loops. For a model with B Transformer Blocks, Full-Stack Looping repeats
the entire sequence of Transformer Blocks L times, while Per-Layer Looping repeats each Transformer Block
L times before proceeding to the next; both therefore have an effective depth of LB. The middle variants
restrict this repeated computation to the middle M Transformer Blocks of the network. Middle-Stack Looping
repeats these M Transformer Blocks together as a stack, whereas Middle-Layer Looping repeats each of these
Transformer Blocks individually. The Transformer Blocks before and after the middle region are used only
once, giving both middle variants an effective depth of B + (L −1)M. Thus, at the same loop factor L, the
middle variants require less training compute because only the middle portion of the network is repeated.
Evaluation.
Each panel fixes model scale and TPP, where TPP denotes training tokens per non-embedding
parameter. We report teacher-forced bits per byte (BPB), with lower values indicating better likelihood. The
L = 1 model is the standard non-looped baseline and is labeled Vanilla. In figures with training FLOPs on
the horizontal axis, marker shape denotes the loop factor. These comparisons capture training compute only;
they do not account for system-level effects such as communication overhead or realized hardware throughput.
Unstable runs.
Some configurations become unstable during training and are excluded by our shared stability
criterion. We mark these runs with red stars in the figures. If the corresponding BPB falls outside the
displayed y-axis range, the star is shown at the edge of the panel only to indicate that the run was unstable;
its vertical position should not be interpreted as the measured BPB. These points are therefore different
from configurations for which an evaluation is simply missing. For example, in the small-model mathematical
and code experiments, 11 of the 80 Both Loop configurations are unstable, mostly at L = 32 and at several
high-TPP 100M settings with L = 8.
E.2
BPB Results on DCLM
How looping behavior changes with scale and loop factor.
Figure 13 and Figure 14 show how the preferred
looping design changes across model scales and as the loop factor L increases. At 70M and 100M, several
designs are competitive at small L, and the strongest choice depends on TPP. The 200M results remain
mixed: Middle-Stack Looping can match or outperform Full-Stack Looping at lower TPP, while full-stack
looping becomes stronger as TPP increases. At 300M and 500M, full-stack looping more consistently reaches
the lowest BPB. At the same time, increasing L shows diminishing returns. In the 70M and 100M sweeps,
which extend to L = 32, the reduction in BPB typically becomes smaller as L grows and can eventually
saturate or reverse, especially for designs that loop the attention and MLP sublayers separately. Full-stack
and Middle-Stack Looping remain more stable over a wider range of L, but their gains also diminish as looping
increases. The 200M–500M experiments extend only to L = 8, so they do not directly establish where these
gains eventually saturate or reverse. However, the marginal reduction in BPB already becomes smaller as L
32


increases, suggesting that further looping would eventually reach a saturation regime rather than continue
providing comparable gains at each increase in loop factor.

Figure 13
DCLM BPB versus loop factor L: 70M and 100M models. Results span TPP 10 to 160 and loop factors
L ∈{1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32}. Each panel fixes model scale and TPP, allowing the effect of increasing the loop factor to be
compared across looping designs. Lower BPB is better; red stars denote unstable runs as described in subsection E.1.
How the compute budget changes the preferred looping design.
Figure 15 and Figure 16 compare DCLM BPB
against total training FLOPs across all model scales. When only a modest increase in training compute is
available, Middle-Stack Looping often provides a larger improvement for the additional FLOPs because only
the middle portion of the network is repeated. With a larger compute budget, Full-Stack Looping can attain
lower absolute BPB, particularly at 300M and 500M, where it more consistently reaches the strongest quality
among the evaluated looping designs. The 70M and 100M results also show that spending progressively more
compute by increasing the loop factor L eventually gives diminishing returns: the BPB reductions become
smaller and can ultimately saturate or reverse. The 200M–500M sweeps extend only to L = 8, but their
marginal gains also tend to shrink as L increases. Together, these results support the same trade-off observed
in the main text: Middle-Stack Looping is often more attractive when additional training compute is limited,
whereas Full-Stack Looping can achieve better quality when more compute is available.

35


E.3
Do the Looping Trends Hold Across Other Datasets?
We next ask whether the trends observed on DCLM persist across other datasets. We first evaluate additional
pretraining corpora: C4 (subsubsection E.3.1), WikiText-103 (subsubsection E.3.2), Dolma v1.5 (subsubsec-
tion E.3.3), RedPajama (subsubsection E.3.4), and Dolma Programming Languages (subsubsection E.3.5).
We then consider more specialized mathematical and code datasets: GSM8K (subsubsection E.3.6), MATH
(subsubsection E.3.7), HumanEval (subsubsection E.3.8), and MBPP (subsubsection E.3.9). Across the
additional pretraining corpora, the same broad pattern observed on DCLM generally persists. At smaller
model scales and lower TPP, full-stack and Middle-Stack Looping are often competitive, while full-stack
looping becomes more consistently favorable as model scale and TPP increase. When training compute is
considered, shallow Middle-Stack Looping remains an attractive alternative because it can achieve substantial
quality gains while repeating only part of the network. The mathematical and code datasets exhibit more
variation across looping designs, but broadly retain the tendency for Full-Stack Looping to become more
favorable at larger scales and higher TPP. Across both groups of datasets, the gains from increasing the loop
factor L also tend to diminish as L grows.
36


E.3.1
C4 English
The C4 sweeps in Figure 17, Figure 18, Figure 19, and Figure 20 closely follow the DCLM pattern: rankings
are more mixed at smaller scales, while Full-Stack Looping becomes more favorable at larger scales and
middle-stack remains attractive for modest compute increments.

Figure 20 C4 English BPB versus total training FLOPs: 200M, 300M, and 500M models. Results span TPP 10 to 100
and loop factors L ∈{1, 2, 4, 8}. Middle-Stack Looping often provides strong gains for a smaller increase in training

E.3.2
WikiText-103
The WikiText-103 results in Figure 21, Figure 22, Figure 23, Figure 24 show the same broad trends observed
on DCLM and C4. At smaller model scales and lower TPP, several looping designs remain competitive, and
the preferred design depends on the training regime and loop factor L. As model scale and TPP increase,
Full-Stack Looping more consistently attains lower BPB. When training compute is considered, however,
shallow Middle-Stack Looping remains attractive because it can achieve substantial quality improvements
with a smaller increase in FLOPs. The gains from increasing L also tend to diminish at larger loop factors.

E.3.3
Dolma v1.5
The Dolma v1.5 results in Figure 25, Figure 26, Figure 27, Figure 28 show the same broad trends observed
on DCLM, C4, and WikiText-103. At smaller model scales and lower TPP, several looping designs remain
competitive, and the preferred design depends on the training regime and loop factor L. As model scale and
TPP increase, Full-Stack Looping more consistently attains lower BPB. When training compute is considered,
however, shallow Middle-Stack Looping remains attractive because it can provide substantial quality gains
with a smaller increase in FLOPs. The benefit from increasing L also tends to diminish at larger loop factors.

E.3.5
Dolma Programming Languages
The Dolma programming-language results in Figure 33, Figure 34, Figure 35, and Figure 36 show a similar
overall pattern, although the differences between looping designs are more pronounced at lower training
budgets. At smaller model scales and lower TPP, Middle-Stack Looping is often especially competitive with
Full-Stack Looping. As model scale and TPP increase, Full-Stack Looping more consistently attains lower
BPB, particularly at 500M. When training compute is considered, shallow Middle-Stack Looping remains
attractive because it can provide strong quality gains with a smaller increase in FLOPs. As on the other
corpora, the improvement from increasing the loop factor L also tends to diminish at larger loop factors.

51


E.3.6
GSM8K
The GSM8K results in Figure 37 and Figure 38 show more variation across looping designs than the general
pretraining corpora. At smaller model scales and lower TPP, several designs remain competitive, with no
single design consistently attaining the lowest BPB. As model scale and TPP increase, Full-Stack Looping
becomes more favorable, although the trend is less uniform than on DCLM. The smaller-model sweeps also
show diminishing returns as the loop factor L increases: BPB typically improves initially, but the reduction
becomes smaller at larger L and can eventually saturate or reverse. These results measure teacher-forced
likelihood on GSM8K rather than generated-answer accuracy.

E.3.7
MATH
The MATH results in Figure 39 and Figure 40 show a clearer preference for Full-Stack Looping than GSM8K,
particularly as model scale and TPP increase. At smaller scales and lower TPP, several looping designs remain
competitive, while at larger scales and higher TPP full-stack looping more consistently attains lower BPB.
The small-model sweeps also show diminishing returns as the loop factor L increases: the reduction in BPB
generally becomes smaller at larger L, and deeper looping can eventually cease to improve quality. These
results measure teacher-forced likelihood on MATH rather than mathematical answer accuracy.

E.3.8
HumanEval
The HumanEval results in Figure 41 and Figure 42 show that the preferred looping design changes with
model scale and TPP. At smaller scales and lower TPP, Full-Stack Looping is not consistently better than the
alternatives; it becomes more favorable as scale and TPP increase. The deeper small-model sweeps also show
diminishing returns: additional loops can eventually stop reducing BPB or reverse earlier gains. These results
measure teacher-forced likelihood on reference code, not whether generated programs pass the HumanEval
tests.

E.3.9
MBPP
The MBPP results in Figure 43 and Figure 44 show substantial variation across looping designs, particularly
at smaller scales and lower TPP. Full-Stack Looping becomes more favorable as model scale and TPP increase,
but the preference is less consistent than on the general pretraining corpora. The small-model sweeps also
exhibit diminishing returns as the loop factor L increases: additional looping produces progressively smaller
reductions in BPB and can eventually cease to improve quality. These results measure teacher-forced likelihood
on MBPP code rather than execution accuracy or pass@k.

59


E.4
Looping at 1B Scale
We test whether the quality–compute trade-off observed at smaller scales persists at 1B parameters across
DCLM (subsubsection E.4.1), C4 (subsubsection E.4.2), Dolma v1.5 (subsubsection E.4.3), Dolma programming
languages (subsubsection E.4.4), RedPajama (subsubsection E.4.5), and WikiText-103 (subsubsection E.4.6).
We focus on the designs that were most competitive in the smaller-scale sweeps: full-stack and middle-stack
looping, together with Per-Layer Looping at shallow loop counts. At TPP 10 and 40, we compare these
designs at L = 2 and L = 4 against the non-looped L = 1 model. The corpus-specific loop-count and
training-FLOP comparisons appear in Figure 45–Figure 56. Across these corpora, the same broad conclusion
holds: looping generally lowers BPB; Full-Stack Looping tends to achieve the lowest BPB at a matched loop
count, while Middle-Stack Looping obtains gains with fewer training FLOPs. Thus, even at 1B, the preferred
schedule depends on whether the priority is quality at a fixed stored parameter count or the cost of additional
computation.
E.4.1
DCLM
On DCLM, all three looping schedules improve over the non-looped model, and Full-Stack Looping attains
the lowest BPB at matched loop counts (Figure 45). Middle-Stack Looping uses fewer additional training
FLOPs, while Full-Stack Looping can spend more compute to reach lower BPB (Figure 46).

F
Additional Results for Loop-Specific Specialization
This section provides the additional results supporting subsection 3.3. The main text uses 300M full-stack
models on DCLM to show that loop-specific corrections become more useful as the number of loops increases.
Here, we test whether this conclusion holds across model sizes, training budgets, looping schedules, and
evaluation datasets. subsection F.1 describes the experimental setup and parameter and compute accounting.
subsection F.2 reports the core sweep over 70M–300M models at TPP 10, 40, and 100 on DCLM, C4 English,
WikiText-103, Dolma v1.5, RedPajama, and Dolma Programming Languages. For each corpus, we examine
how the benefit of specialization changes with loop count, how many parameters it adds, how it compares
with adding independently parameterized layers, and how much training compute it requires. subsection F.3
reports teacher-forced likelihood on GSM8K, MATH, HumanEval, and MBPP; subsection F.4 compares
full-stack, per-layer, middle-stack, and Middle-Layer Looping to determine where specialization is useful; and
subsection F.5 tests whether the full-stack result persists at 500M and 1B. All results use bits per byte (BPB),
for which lower is better.
F.1
Experimental Setup
Models and coverage.
The main sweep covers 70M, 100M, 200M, and 300M models at TPP 10, 40, and 100,
with loop counts L ∈{2, 4, 8}. We evaluate full-stack, per-layer, middle-stack, and Middle-Layer Looping.
For every setting, the baseline uses shared weights in every loop, and we compare it with Loop LoRA, Loop
Matrix, and Loop Softmax. We classify a run as unstable and exclude it when its globally averaged training
loss exceeds 5. This criterion excludes only the three specialization variants of the 300M per-layer model at
L = 8 and TPP 100. Their points are omitted, and curves are not drawn across the gaps. The targeted 500M
and 1B experiments use Full-Stack Looping.
Loop-specific specialization.
The baseline reuses the same weight matrix in every loop. Giving every loop a
different full weight matrix would allow specialization but would largely remove the parameter savings from
weight reuse. We instead retain a shared weight matrix and add a small loop-specific low-rank correction.
Definition 11:
Loop-specific specialization.Let W ∈Rdout×din be shared across L loops. Loop ℓuses
W (ℓ) := W + ∆W (ℓ).
Let {(Aj, Bj)}L
j=1 denote L low-rank components, with Aj ∈Rq×din and Bj ∈Rdout×q.
Loop LoRA assigns one component directly to each loop:
∆W (ℓ) := α
q BℓAℓ.
Loop Matrix learns an arbitrary linear combination:
∆W (ℓ) := α
q
L
X
j=1
MℓjBjAj,
M ∈RL×L.
We initialize M to the identity.
Loop Softmax uses the same mixture with nonnegative coefficients that sum to one:
∆W (ℓ) := α
q
L
X
j=1
softmax(Sℓ,:)jBjAj,
S ∈RL×L.
The logits initially favor the component corresponding to each loop.
Loop LoRA uses a single rank-q component, so rank(∆W (ℓ)) ≤q. Loop Matrix and Loop Softmax can instead
65


combine several such components. By subadditivity of matrix rank,
rank

∆W (ℓ)
≤
X
j:Mℓj̸=0
rank(BjAj) ≤Lq.
The mixed variants therefore increase the maximum rank that a loop-specific correction can represent, although
a learned correction need not attain this upper bound. We use q = 8 and unit scaling, α = q, and apply the
corrections to all attention and SwiGLU MLP projections in every looped Transformer block. The shared
weights and corrections are trained jointly from initialization
Parameter and compute accounting.
For one adapted projection, the corrections add Lq(din+dout) parameters,
plus L2 mixing coefficients for Loop Matrix or Loop Softmax. At fixed nominal TPP, these parameters also
slightly increase the token budget because we recompute D = TPP × Pnon-embed for each model. Reported
training FLOPs include the realized token count, repeated Transformer execution, attention-score computation,
and the low-rank corrections.
F.2
Results from 70M to 300M
We evaluate the models on DCLM, C4 English, WikiText-103, Dolma v1.5, RedPajama, Dolma Programming
Languages, GSM8K, MATH, HumanEval, and MBPP.
66


F.2.1
DCLM
As in the main text, we use DCLM as our primary evaluation corpus because it is the largest corpus in our
evaluation suite. Here, we use DCLM to study four questions: how the benefit of specialization changes with
the number of loops, how many additional parameters it requires, how it compares with adding independently
parameterized layers, and how the comparison changes when training FLOPs are taken into account. We
evaluate 70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100, with the available physical-depth comparison
also including 500M models.
Loop count.
The same loop-count pattern appears at TPP 10, 40, and 100. Specialization usually provides
only a small improvement at L = 2, while its advantage over using identical weights becomes clearer at
L = 4 and L = 8. The three specialization methods remain closer to one another than to the identical-weight
baseline, although Loop Matrix is most often strongest. Figure 57 reports the complete loop-count comparison.

Figure 57 Specialization helps more as the number of loops increases, consistently across TPP. DCLM BPB versus loop count
for 70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. Specialization provides a small improvement at L = 2 and
clearer gains at L = 4 and L = 8. Increasing TPP also generally lowers BPB. Lower is better.
Stored parameters.
Across all three TPP values, loop-specific corrections improve BPB while adding relatively
few stored parameters. Specialization is therefore most attractive when the number of parameters that can be
stored is constrained. The corresponding parameter comparison appears in Figure 58.
Loop-specific specialization or physical depth.
To place the parameter savings from specialization in context,
we compare against a doubled-depth Transformer. This reference is a conventional Transformer with twice as
many blocks as the corresponding baseline, with each block using its own independently learned parameters.
It therefore adds depth by storing new layers rather than by repeatedly applying the same layers. Across
the evaluated TPP values, the doubled-depth model can achieve lower BPB, but requires substantially more
stored parameters. Figure 59 compares these choices directly. Physical-depth references are available for
100M–500M rather than for the complete 70M–300M grid used in the other views.
Training FLOPs.
This comparison includes the training cost of running the additional loops and their loop-
specific corrections. At a similar number of training FLOPs, a Transformer with twice as many independently
parameterized layers can achieve lower BPB across the evaluated TPP values. Therefore, if training compute

Figure 58 Specialization improves DCLM BPB with relatively few additional parameters across TPP. Results cover 70M–300M
full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. The horizontal axis reports additional non-embedding parameters relative
to the identical-weight model. Higher TPP generally lowers BPB. Lower is better.
is limited but storing more parameters is acceptable, training a deeper Transformer may be a better use of
compute than adding and specializing more loops. The complete comparison is shown in Figure 60.
DCLM summary.
The same qualitative conclusions hold at TPP 10, 40, and 100: specialization provides a
small benefit at L = 2 and a clearer benefit at L = 4 and L = 8; the precise specialization method matters
less than allowing the loops to specialize; and Loop Matrix is most often the strongest choice. Increasing TPP
also generally lowers BPB for both shared and specialized models. Thus, training on more tokens improves
overall quality without changing the main conclusion that specialization becomes more useful as the number
of loops increases. In terms of resources, specialization is most useful when stored parameters are constrained.
If training FLOPs are instead the main constraint and more parameters can be stored, training a deeper
Transformer can provide better quality for the same amount of training compute.

C4 English

evaluate 70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. The doubled-depth comparison also includes

although Loop Matrix is most often strongest. Increasing TPP also generally lowers BPB. Figure 61 reports
the complete loop-count comparison.

Figure 61 Specialization generally helps more as the number of loops increases. C4 English BPB versus loop count for
70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. Specialization is generally more useful at L = 4 and L = 8 than
at L = 2. Increasing TPP also generally lowers BPB. Lower is better.
Stored parameters.
Across all three TPP values, loop-specific corrections improve BPB while adding relatively
few stored parameters. Specialization therefore provides a useful middle ground when parameter storage is
constrained: most weights remain shared, while each loop receives a small correction. The corresponding
comparison appears in Figure 62.
Loop-specific specialization or additional Transformer layers.
To put this parameter saving in context, we use
the same doubled-depth reference as in the DCLM comparison. This reference is a conventional Transformer
with twice as many blocks as the corresponding baseline, and every block has its own independently learned
parameters. It therefore adds depth by storing new layers rather than by applying the same layers multiple
times. Across the evaluated TPP values, the doubled-depth Transformer can achieve lower BPB, but requires
substantially more stored parameters. Figure 63 compares the two choices. The doubled-depth references
cover 100M–500M models rather than the complete 70M–300M grid used in the other comparisons.
Training FLOPs.
The FLOP comparison includes the cost of executing the additional loops and their
loop-specific corrections. At a similar number of training FLOPs, the doubled-depth Transformer can often
achieve lower BPB. Therefore, when training compute is the main constraint and more parameters can be

Figure 62 Specialization improves C4 English BPB with relatively few additional parameters. Results cover 70M–300M
full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. The horizontal axis reports additional non-embedding parameters relative
to the identical-weight model. Higher TPP generally lowers BPB. Lower is better.
stored, training a deeper Transformer may be a better use of compute than adding and specializing more
loops. The complete comparison appears in Figure 64.
C4 English summary.
The C4 English results reproduce the main DCLM pattern. Specialization provides
a small benefit at L = 2 and a clearer benefit at L = 4 and L = 8, while the precise specialization method
matters less than allowing the loops to specialize. Loop Matrix is most often the strongest choice. Increasing
TPP generally lowers BPB for both identical-weight and specialized models, so training on more tokens
improves overall quality without changing the main loop-count pattern. In terms of resources, specialization
is most useful when stored parameters are constrained. If training FLOPs are instead the main constraint
and more parameters can be stored, a deeper Transformer can provide better quality for the same amount of
training compute.

Figure 64 Low parameter overhead does not necessarily mean better training-compute efficiency. C4 English BPB versus
total training FLOPs for 70M–300M models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. Training FLOPs include repeated loop execution,
loop-specific corrections, attention-score computation, and the realized token budget. Lower is better.

F.2.3
WikiText-103
WikiText-103 is a smaller, more curated language-modeling corpus than DCLM. It tests whether the specializa-
tion result transfers beyond large web-text evaluations. We repeat the four comparisons used for DCLM: loop
count, added parameter storage, additional independently parameterized layers, and total training FLOPs. We
evaluate 70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. The doubled-depth comparison also includes
500M models.
Loop count.
The same qualitative loop-count pattern remains visible at TPP 10, 40, and 100, although the
differences are somewhat more variable than on the larger web-text corpora. Specialization is usually modest
at L = 2 and becomes clearer at L = 4 and L = 8. Differences among the three specialization methods are
generally smaller than their differences from the identical-weight baseline, with Loop Matrix often strongest.
Increasing TPP also generally lowers BPB. Figure 65 reports the complete loop-count comparison.

Figure 66 Specialization improves WikiText-103 BPB with relatively few additional parameters. Results cover 70M–300M
full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. The horizontal axis reports additional non-embedding parameters relative
to the identical-weight model. Higher TPP generally lowers BPB. Lower is better.
stored, training a deeper Transformer may be a better use of compute than adding and specializing more
loops. The complete comparison appears in Figure 68.
WikiText-103 summary.
WikiText-103 broadly supports the DCLM conclusion. Specialization provides a
small benefit at L = 2 and a clearer benefit at L = 4 and L = 8, while the precise specialization method
matters less than allowing the loops to specialize. Loop Matrix is most often the strongest choice. Increasing
TPP generally lowers BPB for both identical-weight and specialized models, so training on more tokens
improves overall quality without changing the main loop-count pattern. In terms of resources, specialization
is most useful when stored parameters are constrained. If training FLOPs are instead the main constraint
and more parameters can be stored, a deeper Transformer can provide better quality for the same amount of
training compute.

Figure 68 Low parameter overhead does not necessarily mean better training-compute efficiency. WikiText-103 BPB versus
total training FLOPs for 70M–300M models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. Training FLOPs include repeated loop execution,

78


F.2.4
Dolma v1.5
Dolma v1.5 provides a broad language-modeling evaluation corpus with a different text distribution from
DCLM. It tests whether the observed benefits depend on the composition of the evaluation text.
We
repeat the four comparisons used for DCLM: loop count, added parameter storage, additional independently
parameterized layers, and total training FLOPs. We evaluate 70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40,
and 100. The doubled-depth comparison also includes 500M models.
Loop count.
The same loop-count pattern appears across TPP 10, 40, and 100. Specialization usually
produces only a small improvement at L = 2, while the separation from the identical-weight baseline becomes
clearer at L = 4 and L = 8. The three specialization methods are generally closer to one another than to
the baseline, and Loop Matrix is most often strongest. Increasing TPP also generally lowers BPB. Figure 69
reports the complete loop-count comparison.

Figure 69 Specialization generally helps more as the number of loops increases. Dolma v1.5 BPB versus loop count for
70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. Specialization is generally more useful at L = 4 and L = 8 than
at L = 2. Increasing TPP also generally lowers BPB. Lower is better.
Stored parameters.
Across all three TPP values, loop-specific corrections improve BPB while adding relatively
few stored parameters. Specialization therefore provides a useful middle ground when parameter storage is
constrained: most weights remain shared, while each loop receives a small correction. The corresponding
comparison appears in Figure 70.
Loop-specific specialization or additional Transformer layers.
To put this parameter saving in context, we use
the same doubled-depth reference as in the DCLM comparison. This reference is a conventional Transformer
with twice as many blocks as the corresponding baseline, and every block has its own independently learned
parameters. It therefore adds depth by storing new layers rather than by applying the same layers multiple
times. Across the evaluated TPP values, the doubled-depth Transformer can achieve lower BPB, but requires
substantially more stored parameters. Figure 71 compares the two choices. The doubled-depth references
cover 100M–500M models rather than the complete 70M–300M grid used in the other comparisons.
Training FLOPs.
The FLOP comparison includes the cost of executing the additional loops and their
loop-specific corrections. At a similar number of training FLOPs, the doubled-depth Transformer can often
achieve lower BPB. Therefore, when training compute is the main constraint and more parameters can be

F.2.5
RedPajama
RedPajama provides another broad language-modeling evaluation corpus with a text distribution distinct from
both DCLM and Dolma. It gives an additional test of whether the result transfers across general-language
corpora. We repeat the four comparisons used for DCLM: loop count, added parameter storage, additional
independently parameterized layers, and total training FLOPs. We evaluate 70M–300M full-stack models at
TPP 10, 40, and 100. The doubled-depth comparison also includes 500M models.
Loop count.
The results again show only a small benefit from specialization at L = 2 and a clearer benefit at
L = 4 and L = 8, across TPP 10, 40, and 100. The precise method matters less than allowing the loops to
differ, although Loop Matrix most frequently reaches the lowest BPB. Increasing TPP also generally improves
quality. Figure 73 reports the complete loop-count comparison.

Figure 73 Specialization generally helps more as the number of loops increases. RedPajama BPB versus loop count for
70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. Specialization is generally more useful at L = 4 and L = 8 than
at L = 2. Increasing TPP also generally lowers BPB. Lower is better.
Stored parameters.
Across all three TPP values, loop-specific corrections improve BPB while adding relatively
few stored parameters. Specialization therefore provides a useful middle ground when parameter storage is
constrained: most weights remain shared, while each loop receives a small correction. The corresponding
comparison appears in Figure 74.
Loop-specific specialization or additional Transformer layers.
To put this parameter saving in context, we use
the same doubled-depth reference as in the DCLM comparison. This reference is a conventional Transformer
with twice as many blocks as the corresponding baseline, and every block has its own independently learned
parameters. It therefore adds depth by storing new layers rather than by applying the same layers multiple
times. Across the evaluated TPP values, the doubled-depth Transformer can achieve lower BPB, but requires
substantially more stored parameters. Figure 75 compares the two choices. The doubled-depth references
cover 100M–500M models rather than the complete 70M–300M grid used in the other comparisons.
Training FLOPs.
The FLOP comparison includes the cost of executing the additional loops and their
loop-specific corrections. At a similar number of training FLOPs, the doubled-depth Transformer can often
achieve lower BPB. Therefore, when training compute is the main constraint and more parameters can be

Figure 74 Specialization improves RedPajama BPB with relatively few additional parameters. Results cover 70M–300M
full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. The horizontal axis reports additional non-embedding parameters relative
to the identical-weight model. Higher TPP generally lowers BPB. Lower is better.
stored, training a deeper Transformer may be a better use of compute than adding and specializing more
loops. The complete comparison appears in Figure 76.
RedPajama summary.
RedPajama again supports the same resource-dependent conclusion. Specialization
provides a small benefit at L = 2 and a clearer benefit at L = 4 and L = 8, while the precise specialization
method matters less than allowing the loops to specialize. Loop Matrix is most often the strongest choice.
Increasing TPP generally lowers BPB for both identical-weight and specialized models, so training on
more tokens improves overall quality without changing the main loop-count pattern. In terms of resources,
specialization is most useful when stored parameters are constrained. If training FLOPs are instead the main
constraint and more parameters can be stored, a deeper Transformer can provide better quality for the same
amount of training compute.

F.2.6
Dolma Programming Languages
Dolma Programming Languages is a code-heavy evaluation corpus, so it tests whether the specialization result
extends beyond general-language corpora. We repeat the four comparisons used for DCLM: loop count, added
parameter storage, additional independently parameterized layers, and total training FLOPs. We evaluate
70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. The doubled-depth comparison also includes 500M
models.
Loop count.
The code results show the same broad tendency, but with more variation than on the general-
language corpora. Improvements at L = 2 are small or inconsistent, while specialization is more often useful
at L = 4 and L = 8. The three methods usually remain close, and Loop Matrix is often strong, but no method
is uniformly best in every setting. Increasing TPP generally lowers BPB. Figure 77 reports the complete
loop-count comparison.

Figure 77 Specialization generally helps more as the number of loops increases. Dolma Programming Languages BPB versus
loop count for 70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. Specialization is generally more useful at L = 4
and L = 8 than at L = 2. Increasing TPP also generally lowers BPB. Lower is better.
Stored parameters.
When specialization improves code BPB, it does so with only a small increase in stored
parameters. Specialization therefore provides a useful middle ground when parameter storage is constrained:
most weights remain shared, while each loop receives a small correction. The corresponding comparison
appears in Figure 78.
Loop-specific specialization or additional Transformer layers.
To put this parameter saving in context, we use
the same doubled-depth reference as in the DCLM comparison. This reference is a conventional Transformer
with twice as many blocks as the corresponding baseline, and every block has its own independently learned
parameters. It therefore adds depth by storing new layers rather than by applying the same layers multiple
times. The doubled-depth Transformer can achieve lower BPB in several settings, but requires substantially
more stored parameters. Figure 79 compares the two choices. The doubled-depth references cover 100M–500M
models rather than the complete 70M–300M grid used in the other comparisons.
Training FLOPs.
The FLOP comparison includes the cost of executing the additional loops and their
loop-specific corrections. At comparable training FLOPs, the doubled-depth Transformer can be stronger in
several settings. Therefore, when training compute is the main constraint and more parameters can be stored,

Figure 78 Specialization can improve Dolma Programming Languages BPB with relatively few additional parameters. Results
cover 70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. The horizontal axis reports additional non-embedding
parameters relative to the identical-weight model. Higher TPP generally lowers BPB. Lower is better.
training a deeper Transformer may be a better use of compute than adding and specializing more loops. The
complete comparison appears in Figure 80.
Dolma Programming Languages summary.
The code-heavy evaluation broadly supports the same conclusion,
although its gains are less uniform. Specialization is more often useful at L = 4 and L = 8 than at L = 2, but
the improvements vary more across model size and TPP than on the general-language corpora. Increasing
TPP generally lowers BPB for both identical-weight and specialized models, so training on more tokens
improves overall quality without changing the main loop-count pattern. In terms of resources, specialization
is most useful when stored parameters are constrained. If training FLOPs are instead the main constraint
and more parameters can be stored, a deeper Transformer can provide better quality for the same amount of
training compute.

Figure 80 Low parameter overhead does not necessarily mean better training-compute efficiency. Dolma Programming
Languages BPB versus total training FLOPs for 70M–300M models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. Training FLOPs include

90


F.3
Mathematical and Code Likelihood Evaluations from 70M to 300M
We next test whether the specialization findings extend from broad language-modeling corpora to mathematical
reasoning and code. We evaluate the same 70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100, with loop
counts L ∈{2, 4, 8}. For GSM8K and MATH, BPB is computed by teacher-forcing the reference solution.
For HumanEval and MBPP, BPB is computed by teacher-forcing the reference code. As these datasets are
smaller and more specialized than the language-modeling corpora, their comparisons vary more across model
size, TPP, and loop count.
F.3.1
GSM8K
GSM8K tests whether the conclusions from the language-modeling corpora extend to mathematical solution
text. We evaluate 70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. These results measure teacher-forced
likelihood of the reference solution, not final-answer accuracy.
Loop count.
Increasing model size and TPP reliably lowers absolute GSM8K BPB. The effect of loop-specific
specialization is less regular. It often helps at L = 4 or L = 8, whereas at L = 2 the specialized and
identical-weight models frequently overlap, and specialization can sometimes be worse. No specialization
method dominates throughout the grid. GSM8K therefore supports a benefit from specialization in many
configurations, but not a uniform improvement at every model size, TPP value, or loop count. The complete
comparison appears in Figure 81.
Stored parameters and additional Transformer layers.
Loop-specific corrections occupy only the low-overhead
portion of the parameter axis. Within this range, they sometimes improve BPB substantially, but the gain
remains configuration-dependent. The doubled-depth Transformer, which has twice as many blocks with
independently learned weights, achieves lower BPB than the looped models in every displayed model-size
and TPP panel. It also requires substantially more stored parameters. Specialization therefore provides a
storage-efficient middle ground, whereas additional independently parameterized layers are stronger when
their storage cost is acceptable. The complete comparison appears in Figure 82.
Training FLOPs.
The FLOP view reinforces the variability of the GSM8K result. Higher TPP and larger
models lower absolute BPB, but spending more compute on additional loops does not produce a monotonic
improvement in every panel. At a fixed loop count, specialization can outperform identical weights, although
the best method and the size of the gain vary across model size and TPP. The doubled-depth Transformer
achieves lower BPB than the looped alternatives at comparable training FLOPs. Thus, when training
FLOPs are constrained and the additional parameters can be stored, doubling the number of independently
parameterized layers is a better use of compute than adding and specializing more loops. The complete
comparison appears in Figure 83.
GSM8K summary.
Model scale and TPP improve GSM8K likelihood much more consistently than loop-
specific specialization. Specialization helps in many settings, especially among the higher-loop configurations,
but the preferred method and the size of the gain vary. Its clearest advantage is low parameter overhead:
the doubled-depth Transformer achieves lower BPB throughout the parameter comparison, but requires
substantially more storage. Consequently, specialization is useful when stored parameters are constrained,
whereas doubling depth is preferable when training FLOPs are constrained and the additional parameters can
be stored.

F.3.2
MATH
MATH provides a second evaluation of mathematical solution text and tests whether the GSM8K variability
persists on a different reasoning corpus. We again evaluate 70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40,
and 100. These teacher-forced BPB measurements evaluate the reference-solution likelihood rather than
final-answer accuracy.
Loop count.
Increasing model size and TPP consistently lowers MATH BPB. Additional loops also generally
improve likelihood, and loop-specific specialization usually provides a further reduction relative to identical
weights. The benefit is small or mixed in some 70M settings, but becomes more consistent at 100M–300M
and is most visible at L = 4 and L = 8. Loop Matrix is frequently strongest, although differences among
the three methods are usually smaller than their common improvement over identical weights. The complete
comparison appears in Figure 84.
Stored parameters and additional Transformer layers.
Across model sizes and TPP values, loop-specific
corrections usually improve MATH BPB while adding only a small number of parameters. The doubled-depth
Transformer has twice as many blocks, each with independently learned weights. It achieves the lowest BPB
in every displayed panel, but lies far beyond the specialization methods on the parameter axis. Specialization
is therefore attractive when stored parameters are limited; additional independently parameterized layers
remain stronger when their storage cost is acceptable. The complete comparison appears in Figure 85.
Training FLOPs.
Unlike GSM8K, MATH BPB generally decreases as training FLOPs increase through
additional loops. At the same loop count, specialization then provides a smaller but comparatively consistent
improvement over identical weights, particularly at L = 4 and L = 8. The dominant absolute improvements
still come from model scale, TPP, and the additional loop computation; the loop-specific corrections refine
those gains. Even so, the doubled-depth Transformer achieves lower BPB at comparable training FLOPs. If
training FLOPs are the primary constraint and more parameters can be stored, doubling depth is therefore
preferable to adding and specializing more loops. The complete comparison appears in Figure 86.
MATH summary.
MATH provides more consistent evidence for loop-specific specialization than GSM8K.
The benefit is clearest at L = 4 and L = 8 for 100M–300M models and persists across TPP values, while Loop
Matrix is frequently strongest. Higher TPP and larger model size remain the most reliable sources of absolute
improvement. Specialization offers a favorable storage trade-off, whereas the doubled-depth Transformer
achieves lower BPB with substantially more parameters. Thus, specialization is preferable under a storage
constraint, while doubling depth is preferable when training FLOPs are constrained and the additional
parameters can be stored.

F.3.3
HumanEval
HumanEval tests whether the specialization behavior extends to code. We evaluate 70M–300M full-stack
models at TPP 10, 40, and 100 by teacher-forcing the reference implementation. BPB therefore measures
reference-code likelihood, not whether sampled programs pass the unit tests, and should not be interpreted as
execution accuracy or pass@k.
Loop count.
Across model sizes and TPP values, at least one specialization method usually improves on the
corresponding identical-weight model. The separation is generally clearest at L = 4 or L = 8, but neither
the best loop count nor the best method is uniform across the grid. In particular, the low-TPP results are
sometimes non-monotonic in loop count. Increasing TPP substantially lowers absolute BPB without removing
this configuration dependence. The complete comparison appears in Figure 87.
Stored parameters and additional Transformer layers.
The loop-specific corrections add relatively few stored
parameters. The doubled-depth Transformer instead has twice as many blocks, with independently learned
weights in every block. It attains lower HumanEval BPB in every displayed model-size and TPP panel, but
requires a much larger increase in storage. Specialization is therefore a storage-efficient way to improve the
identical-weight model, rather than a replacement for additional depth when those parameters can be stored.
The complete comparison appears in Figure 88.
Training FLOPs.
Within the looped models, spending more training compute through additional loops
generally lowers HumanEval BPB. Specialization often improves on identical weights at the same loop count,
but the size of the gain and the strongest method vary across model size and TPP. At comparable training
FLOPs, however, the doubled-depth Transformer achieves lower BPB. When training FLOPs are constrained
and its additional parameters can be stored, doubling depth is therefore a better use of compute than adding
and specializing more loops. The complete comparison appears in Figure 89.
HumanEval summary.
Larger models and higher TPP consistently improve absolute HumanEval likelihood.
Specialization provides an additional benefit in most configurations, especially at larger loop counts, although
the preferred method varies. The doubled-depth Transformer reaches lower BPB throughout the parameter
comparison, at a substantially larger storage cost. These conclusions concern teacher-forced code likelihood
only. Specialization is the better choice when stored parameters are constrained; doubling depth is the better
choice when training FLOPs are constrained and more parameters can be stored.

Figure 89 At comparable training FLOPs, doubling depth gives lower HumanEval BPB than adding and specializing more loops.
HumanEval BPB versus total training FLOPs for 70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. Training

102


F.3.4
MBPP
MBPP provides a second code-likelihood evaluation and tests whether the HumanEval conclusions persist on
another code corpus. We evaluate the same 70M–300M full-stack models at TPP 10, 40, and 100. MBPP
BPB is computed by teacher-forcing the reference code and should not be interpreted as execution accuracy
or pass@k.
Loop count.
Specialization improves on identical weights in most of the MBPP grid, with the largest-loop
configurations frequently attaining the lowest BPB. The trend is not universal: the best point can occur at
L = 4, and identical weights remain competitive in a few panels. The ranking among Loop Matrix, Loop
LoRA, and Loop Softmax also changes with model size and TPP. By contrast, increasing TPP clearly reduces
absolute BPB at every model size. The complete comparison appears in Figure 90.
Stored parameters and additional Transformer layers.
The loop-specific corrections add relatively few stored
parameters. From 100M through 300M, the doubled-depth Transformer, which has twice as many independently
parameterized blocks, achieves lower MBPP BPB in every displayed TPP column. The 70M comparison
is more mixed: specialization is slightly stronger at TPP 40 and 100, while doubled depth is stronger at
TPP 10. Thus, the quality advantage of additional depth is common but not universal on MBPP, whereas
the parameter savings from specialization hold throughout the grid. The complete comparison appears in
Figure 91.
Training FLOPs.
Increasing the number of loops generally lowers MBPP BPB despite the additional training
FLOPs. Loop-specific corrections frequently improve on identical weights at the same loop count, although
the improvement is not present in every panel and no method dominates the complete grid. At comparable
training FLOPs, the doubled-depth Transformer achieves lower BPB than the looped alternatives. Thus,
when training FLOPs are constrained and the additional parameters can be stored, doubling depth is the
better choice. The complete comparison appears in Figure 92.
MBPP summary.
MBPP supports the same qualified conclusion as HumanEval: loop-specific specialization
usually improves identical weights, particularly at larger loop counts, but the preferred loop count and method
depend on model size and TPP. Additional independently parameterized depth is usually stronger in absolute
likelihood, while specialization requires substantially less storage and can be competitive at 70M. These results
describe teacher-forced likelihood, not generated-code correctness. Specialization is preferable when stored
parameters are constrained; doubling depth is preferable when training FLOPs are constrained and more
parameters can be stored.

F.4
Where Is Loop-Specific Specialization Useful?
The preceding sections use Full-Stack Looping to compare loop count, parameter storage, additional depth,
and training FLOPs within a common architecture. They show that specialization generally improves quality,
with its benefits becoming clearest at higher TPP and larger loop counts. We now ask whether the same
benefit appears when computation is looped elsewhere in the Transformer. We compare full-stack, per-layer,
middle-stack, and Middle-Layer Looping in a representative setting: 300M models at TPP 40, with loop
counts L ∈{2, 4, 8}, evaluated on all six language-modeling corpora. Figure 93, Figure 94, and Figure 95 show
a consistent hierarchy. Full-Stack Looping achieves the strongest absolute quality and receives the largest
gains from specialization. Middle-stack and Middle-Layer Looping can also benefit, but generally by less.
Per-Layer Looping is the clearest exception: loop-specific corrections do not improve it consistently. The
differences among looping schedules are also larger than the differences among Loop LoRA, Loop Matrix,
and Loop Softmax. Thus, choosing where to loop has a larger effect than choosing the precise specialization
method, although specialization provides an additional benefit for the stronger schedules.

Takeaway.
Loop placement is the more important design choice. Full-Stack Looping gives the strongest
quality and benefits most from loop-specific specialization, particularly at larger loop counts and higher
TPP. Middle-stack and Middle-Layer Looping receive smaller gains, while Per-Layer Looping does not
benefit consistently. Once a strong looping schedule has been selected, specialization provides an additional
improvement; the precise choice among Loop LoRA, Loop Matrix, and Loop Softmax is comparatively less
important.
108


F.5
Does Specialization Persist at 500M and 1B?
We finally ask whether the specialization result from the 70M–300M sweep persists at larger model scales. At
500M, we evaluate Loop LoRA, Loop Matrix, and Loop Softmax with L ∈{2, 4, 8} at TPP 10, 40, and 100.
At 1B, all three methods are available through L = 8 at TPP 10 and 40 and through L = 4 at TPP 100; the
L = 8, TPP-100 runs are unavailable. Missing observations are left blank, and lines are not connected across
them.
Across DCLM, C4 English, WikiText-103, Dolma v1.5, RedPajama, and Dolma Programming Languages, the
earlier conclusion continues to hold. Loop-specific specialization generally improves identical-weight full-stack
looping, and its benefit remains clearest at larger loop counts. Differences among Loop LoRA, Loop Matrix,
and Loop Softmax are usually smaller than their shared improvement over identical weights, although the
absolute BPB gains are generally smaller than in the 70M–300M sweep. Thus, the value of allowing loops to
specialize does not disappear as model size increases.
This extension focuses on one question: whether loop-specific specialization continues to help as the model
becomes larger.
The parameter and FLOP views quantify the additional storage and training cost of
the corrections. We believe that increasing the parameter count, for example by adding independently
parameterized Transformer layers, would also improve quality. Here, however, we isolate whether loop-specific
specialization remains useful as model size increases while keeping the Full-Stack Looping design fixed. We do
not have 500M or 1B results on GSM8K, MATH, HumanEval, or MBPP, so the scale conclusion is limited to
the six language-modeling corpora.
F.5.1
DCLM
The DCLM results retain the same broad pattern observed at smaller scales: specialization generally improves
identical-weight full-stack looping, with clearer gains at the larger loop counts. The three views are reported
in Figure 96, Figure 97, and Figure 98.
Dataset takeaway.
Even at 500M and 1B, loop-specific specialization generally improves DCLM BPB, with
the clearest gains at larger loop counts.

Figure 111 Loop-specific specialization remains useful at 500M and 1B on Dolma Programming Languages. Full-stack results

Dataset takeaway.
The same conclusion holds on Dolma Programming Languages: specialization generally
improves larger looped models, showing that the benefit extends to code-heavy language modeling.
Takeaway.
The main specialization conclusion persists at 500M and 1B: small loop-specific corrections
continue to improve Full-Stack Looping, especially when the same weights are reused more times. The precise
specialization method remains less important than allowing the loops to differ at all, but the gains become
smaller as model scale increases. Thus, specialization remains useful at scale. We believe that increasing the
parameter count, for example by adding independently parameterized Transformer layers, would also improve
quality. The purpose of this experiment, however, is to isolate whether specialization itself continues to help
as the model grows.

G
Additional Results for Parallel Looping
This appendix provides the additional experiments supporting subsection 3.4. As described in subsection B.3,
Parallel Loop Transformers (PLTs) reorganize looping so that, after a short warm-up, the computations for
different loops can be evaluated together during autoregressive decoding. Evaluating several loop inputs with
the same weights can increase arithmetic intensity, the amount of arithmetic performed for each byte read
from memory, because one set of weights can be reused across those inputs. PLT does not remove any of
the loop computations. During training, the model still runs all L loops, and the training FLOPs increase
accordingly. We evaluate model quality and account for training FLOPs here; we do not measure wall-clock
decoding latency or throughput.
Our analysis follows a single diagnostic sequence. We first reconstruct the original PLT design and identify the
quality loss introduced by its shifted transition. We call the rule that combines the current-token representation
with the shifted state the re-injection rule. We then measure these two representations, normalize them before
they are combined, and test whether the combination needs a learned gate or additional sources of context.
Finally, we evaluate the resulting design across model scale, loop count, training budget, evaluation corpus,
stored parameters, and training FLOPs. Unless stated otherwise, quality is teacher-forced bits per byte (BPB)
on the DCLM validation set, and lower is better. We do not fill in missing observations or connect curves
across missing points.
Throughout this appendix, Vanilla (No Looping) denotes the unlooped baseline, and Full-Stack Looping
denotes ordinary same-token looping. For readability, we call our selected parallel design shifted PLT (ours).
It separately normalizes the current-token and shifted recurrent inputs and combines them with a rank-8
linear gate. Its same-token counterpart is the unshifted control; this control cannot run its loops concurrently
and is included only to measure the modeling cost of shifting information across token positions. We reserve
Original PLT for the earlier design that combines cross-loop parallelism, KV sharing, and gated sliding-window
attention (G-SWA). Loop count is denoted by L, and an individual loop is indexed by ℓ.
G.1
Which Part of PLT Causes the Quality Loss?
We use the Cross-Loop Parallelism (CLP) transition defined in Def. 9 and reconstruct PLT one component at
a time. We first separate the source of the recurrent state from the attention-cache construction used around
it.
Definition 12:
Shifted, unshifted, and Full-Stack transitions. Let en be the embedding of token n, let
h(ℓ−1)
n
be its output from loop ℓ−1, and let b(ℓ)
n
be the input to loop ℓ. For ℓ> 1, we compare
shifted:
b(ℓ)
n = en + h(ℓ−1)
n−1 ,
unshifted:
b(ℓ)
n = en + h(ℓ−1)
n
,
Full-Stack:
b(ℓ)
n = h(ℓ−1)
n
.
The shifted transition receives the preceding token’s state, removing the same-token dependency
between loops and allowing their computations to be evaluated together during decoding.
The
unshifted and Full-Stack transitions both use the current token’s preceding-loop state and therefore
execute the loops sequentially. They differ only in whether en is explicitly re-injected at every loop.
We now focus on the different cache construction choices; see subsection B.3 for details.
Definition 13:
PLT cache constructions. All three designs below use the shifted transition in Def. 12;
they differ only in the attention state retained by later loops.
Shifted CLP maintains a separate sequence-wide KV cache for every loop.
122


CLP + KV sharing retains a sequence-wide cache only for the first loop and shares it with all later loops.
Original PLT supplements the shared first-loop cache with a private sliding-window cache for each later
loop and uses gated sliding-window attention (G-SWA) to combine the shared global and loop-local
context.
The cross-token shift is the main source of PLT’s quality loss.
The left panels of Figure 114 and Figure 115
compare these cache constructions using the original, unnormalized re-injection rule. The unshifted control
closely tracks ordinary Full-Stack Looping because both preserve the same-token dependency between successive
loops. This confirms that the shared Transformer benefits from additional loops when the state remains
aligned with the current token. Neither construction, however, provides PLT’s inference-efficiency mechanism:
each token must complete one loop before entering the next.
Raw Shifted CLP behaves very differently. Its quality degrades as the number of loops increases and frequently
fails even to match Vanilla (No Looping). This failure is more severe than the behavior reported in prior work.
The original PLT paper finds gains over a non-looped Transformer (Wu et al., 2025), while LoopCoder-v2
reports a sharp optimum at two total loops: the second loop improves performance, but models with three or
more loops regress (Yang et al., 2026). This behavior is initially surprising: additional loops perform more
computation, yet the model can become worse.
This gap matters because the goal is to obtain the quality benefit of ordinary Full-Stack Looping while
allowing the loops to run concurrently during decoding. The raw shifted transition does not achieve that goal.
KV sharing and G-SWA recover part of the loss, but neither closes the gap to the unshifted and Full-Stack
controls. Because all three PLT variants use the same shifted state, these comparisons point to the way that
state is combined with the current-token embedding, rather than the cache construction alone, as the main
source of the problem. In the next subsection, we examine why this normalization helps and show that the
failure arises from a scale mismatch between the two inputs (see subsection G.2 and subsection G.4). For now,
we focus on the empirical result: separately normalizing the current-token embedding and the shifted state
(see Def. 14) before combining them recovers much of the lost quality.
The right panels in Figure 114 and Figure 115 retain the corresponding recurrent source and cache construction
but change how the recurrent state is combined with the current token.
Definition 14:

Figure 114 The shifted re-injection interface is the main source of PLT’s quality loss at 200M. DCLM BPB at TPP 40 (top)
and TPP 80 (bottom) for L ∈{2, 4, 8}. The left panels isolate the unnormalized PLT designs. The unshifted control
closely tracks sequential Full-Stack Looping, whereas raw Shifted CLP can fail to match even Vanilla. The right panels
show that separately normalizing and then controlling the mixture of the two inputs provides the largest recovery.
Lower BPB is better.
Fixed 50/50 + RMS Norm uses the parameter-free gate g(ℓ)
n

b(ℓ)

Normalized Addition (No Gate) instead computes

Thus, it applies the same separate RMS normalization but directly adds the two streams. Unless a
curve is labeled Normalized Addition, every normalized design in the right panels uses the fixed 50/50
re-injection gate and differs only in its recurrent source or KV-cache construction.
Normalization and controlled mixing repair the shifted interface.
Normalization improves nearly every shifted
construction in the right panels, but Normalized Addition does not match the consistency of the fixed gated
mixture. The unshifted normalized model performs best overall, confirming that same-token state is easiest to
reuse, but it remains sequential during decoding. The normalized shifted model with the fixed 50/50 gate is
generally the strongest parallelizable alternative. The main repair is therefore not RMS normalization alone,
but separately normalizing the two streams and controlling how much each contributes.
124


Normalization closes the quality gap caused by the cross-token shift.
The quality loss is the increase in DCLM
BPB that occurs when ordinary Full-Stack Looping is replaced by the raw shifted PLT transition. As shown
in the left panels, raw Shifted CLP performs worse than Full-Stack Looping and can even perform worse than
Vanilla (No Looping). The right panels retain the same cross-token shift but normalize the current-token
embedding and shifted state before combining them. This substantially lowers BPB across nearly every PLT
variant and brings the shifted models much closer to Full-Stack Looping. Directly adding the two normalized
inputs helps, but averaging them with the fixed 50/50 rule is more reliable. Thus, the main gain comes from
placing the two inputs on comparable scales and controlling how much each contributes, rather than from
removing the cross-token shift.
Parallel looping can recover the quality of ordinary looping.
At both 200M and 300M, the strongest shifted
results generally come from the normalized fixed 50/50 model with a separate KV cache for every loop. In our
settings, this design matches and sometimes slightly outperforms Full-Stack Looping while still allowing the
loops to run concurrently during decoding. The normalized unshifted model can perform even better, but it
does not provide this inference benefit because its loops remain sequential. These comparisons show that using
the preceding token’s state need not sacrifice the quality gains of ordinary looping when the current-token
embedding and shifted state are normalized and combined carefully.
The loop-count trend also differs from LoopCoder-v2 (Yang et al., 2026). That work finds that two total loops
improve over a non-looped model, whereas three or more loops reduce performance. In our normalized shifted
models, quality generally improves or remains stable from L = 2 to L = 4 and L = 8; we do not observe the
same sharp decline after two loops. This suggests that keeping the two inputs at comparable scales helps later
loops remain useful. The two studies use different model sizes, training data, and evaluations, so this is not a
direct comparison, and we do not claim that improvements will continue beyond the loop counts tested here.
KV-cache sharing still trades memory for quality.
Using a separate KV cache for every loop gives the
strongest shifted results, but its memory use grows with L. Sharing only the first loop’s full cache, shown
as CLP + KV Sharing + RMS Norm, performs substantially worse even after normalization. G-SWA offers
a better balance: it combines the shared first-loop cache with a small private window for each later loop,
often approaches the separate-cache model, and can match Full-Stack Looping at larger loop counts. In
the experiments that follow, we use a separate KV cache for every loop so that we can study the effect of
normalization without also changing the information available to attention. The G-SWA results show that
the same normalization can also be combined with a more memory-efficient cache design.
Practical Takeaway
Normalization is the key intervention that makes parallel looping competitive. Separately normalizing the
current-token embedding and the shifted recurrent state allows the shifted model to match Full-Stack
Looping in our best settings while retaining PLT’s inference-time parallelism. When the shift is removed,
the same normalized transition can outperform Full-Stack Looping, although this unshifted control
remains sequential during decoding. Whereas LoopCoder-v2 reports that performance peaks at two
total loops and regresses with three or more (Yang et al., 2026), our normalized shifted models show
neither the same saturation at L = 2 nor systematic regression over L ∈{2, 4, 8}. These results suggest
that stabilizing the scales of the two representations allows later loops to remain useful and that the
two-loop limit is not intrinsic to parallel looping.
These comparisons identify the re-injection interface as the central modeling problem and motivate examining
the scales of the current-token representation and shifted state directly.
G.2
Why Does Normalization Help PLT?
The preceding section showed that raw shifted PLT loses quality relative to Full-Stack Looping, while separately
normalizing its two inputs recovers much of this loss. We now ask why normalization matters. We hypothesize
that the loop representations and embeddings differ substantially in scale. To test this, we measure the
magnitudes of the token embedding and shifted state across training budgets, loops, model sizes, and PLT
cache designs. Raw shifted PLT adds two representations with different roles. The embedding en corresponds

Raw addition gives both inputs a coefficient of one, but this does not make their contributions equal when
their magnitudes differ. If one input is much larger, its scale can strongly influence the result. We therefore
measure whether the two inputs have comparable magnitudes before they are combined.
Measuring the scale difference.
For a token representation xn ∈Rd, we measure its root-mean-square

the models; it is not an additional likelihood evaluation.

The scale difference changes with training budget and loop depth.
We first fix the model size at 100M and
compare different training budgets for models with eight loops. As shown in Figure 116, the hidden states are
consistently much larger than the token embeddings. The magnitude of this difference also changes across
training budgets and loops. Raw addition therefore does not combine the two inputs in a consistent balance:
which input contributes more to the sum can change with both training budget and loop depth. This finding
is consistent with the scale difference contributing to the lower quality of raw shifted PLT. Full-Stack Looping
and normalized shifted PLT are included as references.

Shifted PLT (rank-8 gate + RMS normalization)
Figure 116 The scale difference changes with training budget and loop depth. Hidden-state RMS divided by token-embedding
RMS for final 100M, L = 8 checkpoints evaluated on the same DCLM sample. Each point is the ratio of the mean
hidden-state RMS across tokens to the mean embedding RMS across tokens. The figure compares Full-Stack Looping,
raw shifted PLT, and normalized shifted PLT. The raw shifted model uses neither normalization nor a gate before
combining its inputs. The normalized reference uses the rank-8 linear gate defined in subsection G.3. The y-axis uses
logarithmic spacing, but the tick labels show the underlying RMS ratios, not their logarithms.
The scale difference remains across model sizes and cache designs.
The first experiment could reflect a
particular model size or PLT design. We therefore fix the training budget and compare 100M and 200M
models, including Original PLT with KV sharing and G-SWA. As shown in Figure 117, the hidden states
remain substantially larger than the embeddings at both model sizes and in both raw shifted CLP and Original
PLT. KV sharing and G-SWA change how the difference develops across loops, but do not make the two
inputs similar in scale before they are added. The problem is therefore not limited to one model size, nor is it
removed by changing the cache and attention design. A normalized model can still produce a hidden state
whose RMS is larger than the embedding RMS. We normalize the two inputs only when they are about to be
combined at the next loop; we do not constrain the size of the Transformer’s output. The relevant question
is therefore whether the inputs are placed on comparable scales before they are combined, not whether the
Transformer must produce an output with an RMS ratio of one.
Together with the quality improvements in Figure 114 and Figure 115, these measurements support differences
in input scale as an important contributor to the failure of raw addition. They do not show that scale is the
only cause, but they motivate normalizing the embedding and shifted state immediately before combining
them.
Practical Takeaway
In raw shifted PLT, the embedding and shifted hidden state can differ substantially in scale, and the
size of this mismatch varies with training budget and loop depth. The preceding ablations show that
separate normalization and learned gating both improve model quality. Together, they bring shifted PLT
close to the performance of Full-Stack Looping, while the unshifted PLT variant surpasses Full-Stack
Looping. These results show that PLT can achieve strong performance and that controlling the relative
scales and interaction of the two inputs is particularly important when using the shifted formulation.

128


G.3
Does PLT Need a Learned Gate?
Separate RMS normalization puts the current-token embedding and the shifted hidden state on comparable
scales, but it does not specify how the two should be combined. The embedding anchors the computation to
the current token, while the shifted state carries information produced by the previous loop for the preceding
token. The appropriate balance between them may change across tokens, hidden dimensions, and loops. A
fixed 50/50 mixture always gives them equal weight, whereas a learned gate can adjust their contributions
based on their contents. We test whether this flexibility is useful by keeping the normalization fixed and
varying only the way the two inputs are combined. To determine how much the next loop should use from
each source, we introduce a set of mixing weights between the current-token embedding and the shifted hidden
representation. Recall that ¯en is the normalized embedding of the current token, while ¯r(ℓ)
n
is the normalized
hidden representation produced by the preceding loop for the previous token. Loop ℓreceives
b(ℓ)
n = g(ℓ)
n ⊙¯en +

1 −g(ℓ)
n


the gate because it controls how much information is
taken from each input. For a given hidden dimension, a gate value of one selects the current-token embedding,
a value of zero selects the shifted hidden representation, and a value of one half averages the two. For the
learned gates, we predict these mixing weights from both inputs. We concatenate them as z(ℓ)

= [¯en; ¯r(ℓ)

use z(ℓ)

Gate choice matters less than normalization.

Figure 118 Gate choice has little effect once the inputs are normalized. DCLM BPB for fixed 50/50, scalar, rank-8 linear,
and rank-8 MLP mixing at 100M and 200M. Rows show model size and columns show training budget; each panel
compares loop counts. Insets report matched BPB differences from the normalized fixed 50/50 baseline, with negative
values favoring the learned gate. The rank-8 linear gate improves over fixed 50/50 in most matched settings, but the
differences are small and should be treated as directional because most settings use one training seed. Lower BPB is
better.
and results for the 300M and 500M models appear in Figure 119. Once the embedding and shifted hidden
representation are normalized separately, all four methods perform similarly across model sizes, training
budgets, and loop counts. The rank-8 linear gate performs best in most matched comparisons, but its
improvement over fixed 50/50 mixing is small. We therefore view the results as favoring the rank-8 linear gate
without demonstrating a substantial advantage. More importantly, they show that normalization matters
much more than the particular method used to combine the normalized inputs.
We use the rank-8 linear gate in the experiments that follow, including our 1B models. Although the gate
comparison used to select it covers models from 100M to 500M, its effectiveness at 1B is evaluated separately
in Figure 8. The small differences among the mixing rules suggest that fixed 50/50 mixing would lead to
the same qualitative conclusions. The rank-8 gate adds 25d parameters: 16d for Wdown, 8d for Wup, and d
for the bias. These parameters are shared across loops, so their number does not increase with L. Across
the 100M–1B models in which we use the gate, they account for at most 0.0142% of the non-embedding
parameters. At 1B, the gate adds 51,200 parameters, corresponding to approximately 0.0053% of the model’s
non-embedding parameters. It therefore has a negligible effect on both the stored parameter count and the
training-token budget determined from TPP.
Practical Takeaway
Once the two inputs are normalized, no mixing rule clearly dominates: learned gates provide only
marginal gains and often perform similarly to fixed 50/50 mixing. We use the rank-8 linear gate as a
common default because it allows the mixture to vary across tokens and hidden dimensions, remains
consistently competitive, and adds negligible complexity. We do not interpret this choice as evidence
that the rank-8 gate is essential, and expect fixed 50/50 mixing to yield the same qualitative conclusions.
The gate is shared across loops and adds less than 0.015% to the non-embedding parameter count at
every scale from 100M to 1B.

Figure 119 The gate comparison remains close at larger model sizes. DCLM BPB for the same four mixing rules at 300M
