Having found that the precise gating mechanism has little effect, we keep RMS normalization and the rank-8
linear gate fixed in the remaining ablations. We then ask which information must be provided to each loop.
Specifically, we compare using the preceding loop’s hidden representation from either the previous token or
the current token, and test each case with and without the current-token embedding. These comparisons
separate the effect of PLT’s cross-token shift from the benefit of explicitly supplying information about the
token currently being processed.
131


G.4
Why Does Shifted Looping Need the Current-Token Embedding?
Having fixed how the two inputs are combined, we next ask which inputs each loop needs. Ordinary Full-Stack
Looping passes the hidden representation of the current token from one loop to the next. PLT instead
uses the representation produced by the preceding loop for the previous token and combines it with the
embedding of the current token. This change allows the loops to run concurrently during decoding, but it
also changes the information available to each loop. We separate these two choices by varying whether the
hidden representation comes from the previous or current token and whether the current-token embedding is
included.
When a hidden representation and an embedding are both present, we normalize them separately and combine
them using the rank-8 linear gate selected in subsection G.3. The final condition below is deliberately different:
it omits both normalization and the gate so that it exactly reproduces ordinary Full-Stack Looping. A bar
denotes RMS normalization as defined in Def. 14.
Definition 16: Inputs provided to later loops. For loops ℓ> 1, we compare four choices.
Previous-token representation with the current-token embedding:
b(ℓ)
n = g(ℓ)
n ⊙¯en + (1 −g(ℓ)
n ) ⊙¯h(ℓ−1)
n−1 .
Current-token representation with the current-token embedding:
b(ℓ)
n = g(ℓ)
n ⊙¯en + (1 −g(ℓ)
n ) ⊙¯h(ℓ−1)
n
.
Previous-token representation without the current-token embedding:
b(ℓ)
n = ¯h(ℓ−1)
n−1 .
Current-token representation without the current-token embedding:
b(ℓ)
n = h(ℓ−1)
n
.
The final choice is ordinary Full-Stack Looping: each loop receives the representation produced by the
preceding loop for the same token.
The two choices that use h(ℓ−1)
n−1
retain PLT’s ability to run the loops concurrently during decoding. The
choices that use h(ℓ−1)
n
require the current token to finish one loop before entering the next and therefore
remain sequential.
The Full-Stack control verifies the implementation.
The current-token condition without the embedding is
mathematically equivalent to ordinary Full-Stack Looping. Its performance closely matches our independent
Full-Stack implementation in Figure 120. This confirms that the comparison reproduces ordinary looping
when the cross-token shift and additional embedding are both removed.
The current-token embedding is especially important after the shift.
Including the current-token embedding
improves both the previous-token and current-token variants. Its effect is much larger when the hidden
representation comes from the previous token. Without the embedding, the shifted model performs worse
than Vanilla (No Looping), and several runs diverge. Because the two conditions that combine an embedding
and a hidden representation use the same normalization and gate, this difference isolates the information
provided by the current-token embedding.
Why might the embedding be necessary?
The representation at position n −1 is computed before the model
observes the token at position n. It contains the context used to predict the next token, but it does not
specify which token actually occurred. Passing this representation to the next loop without en therefore asks

Figure 120 Shifted looping requires the current-token embedding. DCLM BPB across training budgets for the four inputs
in Def. 16. The current-token representation without the embedding closely matches the independent Full-Stack
implementation, providing a check of the implementation. Adding the embedding improves both the previous-token
and current-token variants. When the representation comes from the previous token, removing the embedding produces
results worse than Vanilla (No Looping), and several runs diverge. The two conditions with both inputs use separate
RMS normalization and the rank-8 linear gate. Missing or diverged results are not filled in. Lower BPB is better.
the model to continue processing information derived from the previous context without directly telling it
the identity of the current token. Repeating this across several loops may cause the representation to move
progressively farther from the token being processed.
Providing en at every loop gives the model the identity of the observed token and may keep the repeated
computation tied to the current position. We hypothesize that this is why the embedding improves training
stability and quality, particularly as the number of shifted loops increases. The experiment establishes that
the embedding is necessary in our shifted models, although it does not by itself prove this explanation.
Practical Takeaway
Ordinary Full-Stack Looping is recovered when each loop receives only the current token’s representation
from the preceding loop, which verifies our implementation. When that representation instead comes
from the previous token, the current-token embedding becomes essential: including it improves quality,
while removing it performs worse than no looping and can cause training to diverge. We hypothesize that
the previous-token representation reflects what the preceding context predicts, whereas the embedding
tells each loop which token actually occurred.
For the remaining shifted-PLT experiments, we therefore use separate RMS normalization, the rank-8 linear
gate, and the current-token embedding. We next ask whether supplying additional information from earlier
tokens provides any further benefit.
133


H
Evaluating PLT Across Datasets and Resource Budgets
The previous section identified the PLT design used in the remaining experiments: the current-token embedding
and the hidden representation from the preceding loop are normalized separately and combined using the
rank-8 linear gate. We now keep this design fixed and evaluate it more broadly. We compare two versions
throughout. Shifted PLT uses the previous token’s representation and allows the loops to run concurrently
during decoding. Unshifted PLT uses the current token’s representation from the preceding loop and must
run its loops one after another. It therefore serves as a quality reference rather than an inference-efficient
alternative. We first compare the two versions across evaluation datasets at 1B. We then vary model size,
loop count, and training budget on DCLM, before comparing quality against stored parameters and training
FLOPs.
H.1
How Does PLT Perform Across Evaluation Datasets at 1B?
We begin by testing whether the behavior observed on DCLM extends to other evaluation datasets. Figure 121
fixes the model size at 1B and the loop count at L = 2, and compares Vanilla (No Looping), ordinary Full-Stack
Looping, shifted PLT, and unshifted PLT across TPP 10–160. We evaluate the same trained checkpoints on
DCLM, C4 English, WikiText-103, Dolma v1.5, RedPajama, and Dolma programming languages, so only the
evaluation data change across panels. Lower BPB is better.
Across the six datasets, shifted PLT closely matches unshifted PLT and performs better than ordinary
Full-Stack Looping, which in turn performs better than Vanilla (No Looping). Unlike unshifted PLT and
Full-Stack Looping, shifted PLT retains the ability to run its loops concurrently during decoding. The same
pattern at 1B and across different datasets shows that the result is not limited to smaller models or to DCLM.
It also reinforces the conclusion of the preceding section: separately normalizing the two inputs and supplying
the current-token embedding are important for preserving quality when the hidden representation is shifted
across tokens.
H.2
How Does PLT Change with Model Size and Loop Count?
We next return to DCLM and examine how the two PLT variants change with model size and loop count.
Each panel in Figure 122 and Figure 123 fixes the model size and TPP while varying L. The figures compare
Vanilla (No Looping), ordinary Full-Stack Looping, shifted PLT, and unshifted PLT.
The effect of the shift depends on model size and loop count.
Unshifted PLT achieves the lowest BPB
throughout these comparisons. At the smaller model sizes, a gap between shifted and unshifted PLT is already
visible at L = 2, although both generally improve over ordinary Full-Stack Looping and Vanilla (No Looping).
As model size increases, shifted and unshifted PLT become much closer at L = 2. The same convergence is
not apparent at L = 4, where shifted PLT continues to trail the unshifted version.
Why might the gap remain at larger loop counts?
We hypothesize that this difference arises from what each
additional loop is asked to refine. Unshifted PLT passes the current token’s representation from one loop to
the next, so every loop continues refining a representation of the same token. Shifted PLT instead receives
a representation produced for the previous token. This representation summarizes the preceding context
and can be viewed as a prediction of the representation needed at the current position. The current-token
embedding anchors the computation to the token that actually occurred, but this context-based prediction
may still be a noisier starting point than the current token’s own previous-loop representation. Larger models
appear better able to compensate for this at L = 2. At L = 4, however, information is passed across tokens
repeatedly, so small errors may build up and preserve the gap from unshifted PLT.

Figure 124 DCLM quality across training budgets at 100M and 200M. The top row shows 100M and the bottom row
shows 200M. Each panel fixes the loop count and varies TPP from 10 to 160 for Full-Stack Looping, shifted PLT,
and unshifted PLT. Unshifted PLT achieves the lowest BPB, while shifted PLT usually matches or improves upon
Full-Stack Looping. Lower BPB is better.
The results show the same pattern as the loop-count view above. Unshifted PLT achieves the lowest BPB
throughout the evaluated settings. At L = 2, the gap between shifted and unshifted PLT narrows as model
size increases; at the larger scales, shifted PLT closely tracks unshifted PLT across the full TPP range. At
L = 4, a clearer gap remains across training budgets. Increasing model scale therefore reduces the cost of
shifting the hidden representation across tokens when only two loops are used, but does not fully remove it
when the shift is repeated over more loops.
Even with this remaining gap, shifted PLT usually matches or improves upon ordinary Full-Stack Looping
across TPP values. This is important because Full-Stack Looping must run its loops sequentially during
decoding, whereas shifted PLT allows them to run concurrently. These results therefore show that shifted PLT
can retain the quality benefit of ordinary looping while remaining more efficient during inference. They are also
138


consistent with our earlier finding that preserving the current-token embedding and separately normalizing it
and the shifted hidden representation are important for making shifted PLT work.

H.4
Stored Parameters and Training FLOPs
The preceding comparisons fix model size and TPP, but adding loops still increases training FLOPs because
the Transformer must run once for every loop. We therefore compare the models in two ways. First, we hold
TPP fixed and ask how quality changes with stored parameters and training FLOPs. We then allow TPP to
vary and compare the models at similar total training FLOPs.
At fixed TPP, parameters and training FLOPs favor different choices.
The parameter view in Figure 126
shows the main storage benefit of looping. Increasing the number of loops can lower BPB while keeping the
number of stored parameters nearly unchanged because the same weights are reused. If absolute quality is the
main objective among the looped models, unshifted PLT with L ∈{2, 4} is generally the strongest choice in
the evaluated range, although it must run its loops sequentially during decoding. The training-FLOP view
gives a different answer because every additional loop requires another evaluation of the Transformer during
training. If training FLOPs are constrained, using fewer loops, typically L = 2, therefore provides a better
return than L = 4. When additional parameters can also be stored, increasing physical depth by adding
Transformer layers with their own weights generally gives the strongest quality for the training FLOPs used.
This reproduces the trade-off observed for ordinary looping in subsection 3.2: the best choice depends on
whether the primary constraint is stored parameters, training FLOPs, or inference efficiency.

Figure 126 Stored parameters and training FLOPs favor different loop counts. DCLM BPB versus stored non-embedding
parameters (top) and total training FLOPs (bottom). Columns fix TPP, and marker shape denotes loop count.
Looping can improve quality with little additional parameter storage. When training FLOPs are the constraint and
additional parameters can be stored, models made deeper with distinct Transformer layers generally give the stronger
quality–compute trade-off. Lower BPB is better.
Across TPP, two-loop PLT comes close to the measured compute frontier.
Allowing TPP to vary changes
the comparison because each model size is now evaluated under several allocations of training compute.
Figure 127 traces Vanilla (No Looping) and shifted PLT from TPP 10 to 160. With L = 2, shifted PLT
appears to approach the best quality–compute trade-off among the measured models as model size increases.
The unshifted version comes closer still (Figure 128), although it does not establish a new quality–compute
frontier and does not provide parallel decoding.
141


The curves also cross at some operating points. Within the same model scale, a two-loop model trained
at a lower TPP, and therefore on fewer tokens, can outperform a Vanilla model trained at a higher TPP
while using comparable total training FLOPs. We do not interpret this as evidence that PLT is uniformly
more data-efficient. Instead, it shows that, for some model scales and compute budgets, allocating part of
the training compute to a second loop can be more effective than allocating all of it to additional training
tokens. The same behavior appears in the headline comparison in Figure 1. With L = 4, the additional loop
computation generally moves the model farther from the best measured quality–compute trade-off, suggesting
that this benefit is specific to relatively shallow looping over the range studied.

Figure 130 DCLM quality versus total training FLOPs at 300M and 500M. The left plot shows 300M and the right plot

For completeness, Figure 129 and Figure 130 report the training-FLOP comparisons separately for the
100M–500M models, while Figure 131 reports the corresponding 1B results. Each figure includes every
measured TPP value, and the insets report the BPB difference from the corresponding Full-Stack model.
These per-model results support the aggregate trends above. Unshifted PLT generally achieves the best
quality, while shifted PLT with L = 2 becomes more competitive as model size increases and often matches
or improves upon Full-Stack Looping. Using L = 4 can lower BPB further, but generally provides a weaker
return per training FLOP.
Practical Takeaway
For practitioners, the choice depends on the primary constraint. If the goal is the strongest quality
and sequential decoding is acceptable, unshifted PLT with L ∈{2, 4} is generally the strongest looped
design. If decoding efficiency matters, shifted PLT with separate input normalization and L = 2 is a
strong default: it allows the loops to run concurrently, usually matches or improves upon Full-Stack
Looping, and approaches unshifted PLT as model size increases. At some measured operating points, it
also outperforms Vanilla models trained on more tokens at comparable training FLOPs, showing that
shallow looping can improve token efficiency in some regimes. Increasing to L = 4 can further lower
BPB when stored parameters are limited, but generally provides a weaker return per training FLOP.
If training FLOPs are the main constraint and additional parameters can be stored, adding distinct
Transformer layers is typically preferable to increasing the number of loops.

I
Parallel Looping with Loop-Specific Specialization
The preceding sections study loop-specific specialization and parallel looping separately. Here, we provide a
short supporting experiment asking whether the two can be used together. We focus on Loop Matrix because
it was generally the strongest of the specialization methods compared in subsection 3.3. We use the same
method defined in Def. 11, without introducing a new specialization design. The figures label the same
method as Matrix LoRA; throughout the text, we use Loop Matrix. Its loop-specific corrections are added
to the attention and MLP projections and trained jointly with the rest of the model. We compare three
forms of looping. Full-Stack Looping is ordinary looping, in which each token passes through the complete
Transformer repeatedly. Shifted PLT passes information across adjacent token positions and allows the loops
to run concurrently during autoregressive decoding. Unshifted PLT passes the current token’s representation
directly from one loop to the next. It provides a useful quality reference, but its loops remain sequential
during decoding. We first examine DCLM in detail and then check the same pattern across DCLM and five
additional language-modeling corpora.
I.1
DCLM Results
Figure 132 compares 300M and 500M models at TPP 10–80 and L ∈{2, 4}. The shared-weight results
are included for context, while the Loop Matrix results show what happens when the loops are allowed to
specialize. The ordering is consistent with the preceding PLT experiments. Unshifted PLT achieves the lowest
BPB, but remains sequential during decoding. Shifted PLT is generally stronger than ordinary Full-Stack
looping while retaining PLT’s parallel decoding schedule. Adding Loop Matrix does not change this overall
ordering.
The effect of specialization also follows the pattern observed in subsection 3.3. Its benefit is small or mixed
at L = 2 and becomes clearer at L = 4, when the same Transformer weights are reused more times. Thus,
loop-specific specialization remains useful in PLT, but the central comparison here is architectural: shifted
PLT retains its advantage over ordinary Full-Stack Looping after the same specialization is applied to both.
I.2
Results Across Six Language-Modeling Corpora
We next test whether the DCLM result in Figure 132 is specific to one evaluation corpus. We report C4
English in Figure 133, WikiText-103 and Dolma v1.5 in Figure 134, and RedPajama and Dolma programming
languages in Figure 135. Each figure uses the same 300M and 500M models, TPP 10–80, and L ∈{2, 4}
comparison as the DCLM figure. The figures show ordinary Full-Stack Looping, shifted PLT, and unshifted
PLT, both with shared weights and with Loop Matrix. The same broad pattern appears across the six corpora.
Unshifted PLT is usually strongest but remains sequential during decoding. Shifted PLT generally improves
over ordinary Full-Stack Looping while retaining parallel decoding. Loop Matrix also generally improves
quality, with the clearest and most consistent gains at L = 4. At L = 2, its effect is smaller and can be mixed,
particularly on the code-heavy Dolma programming-languages split (Figure 135). Taken together, Figure 133,
Figure 134, and Figure 135 show that the DCLM conclusion transfers across evaluation corpora: specialization
generally helps when the weights are reused more deeply, and it does not remove the advantage of shifted
PLT over ordinary Full-Stack Looping.
Overall, the cross-corpus results show that loop-specific specialization and parallel looping remain complemen-
tary, particularly when the same weights are reused across more loops.
Practical Takeaway
Across the six language-modeling corpora, Loop Matrix generally improves quality, with clearer and
more consistent gains at L = 4 than at L = 2. Unshifted PLT usually gives the strongest quality but
remains sequential during decoding. When parallel decoding is required, shifted PLT with Loop Matrix
is the more useful choice: it generally outperforms ordinary Full-Stack Looping while preserving PLT’s
parallel decoding schedule.

Figure 132 Loop-specific specialization preserves the ordering among ordinary and parallel looping designs. DCLM BPB for
300M and 500M models. The figure compares ordinary Full-Stack Looping, shifted PLT, and unshifted PLT with
shared weights and with Loop Matrix. Unshifted PLT gives the strongest quality but remains sequential during
decoding. Shifted PLT generally improves over Full-Stack Looping while retaining parallel decoding. Lower BPB is
better.
J
Limitations
Our experiments establish consistent looping trends across dense decoder-only transformers trained from
scratch at scales up to 1B parameters. We do not evaluate larger models, mixture-of-experts architectures, or
post-trained models; future work can test how broadly these trends extend. We study a diverse set of looping
strategies, low-rank specialization methods, training budgets, and evaluation datasets, but do not exhaust
the space of possible designs. We also focus primarily on likelihood-based evaluation rather than free-form
generation. Future work can broaden both the architectural and evaluation settings. Our normalized shifted
PLT performs particularly well at L = 2 and remains competitive at L = 4. We do not establish whether
this behavior persists at substantially larger loop counts and model scales. Closing the remaining gap to the
unshifted control and developing a theoretical account of how normalization and current-token embedding
reinjection affect stability are promising directions for future work.
146

Figure 133 Parallel looping with Loop Matrix on C4 English. Absolute BPB for ordinary Full-Stack Looping, shifted PLT,
and unshifted PLT with shared weights and with Loop Matrix. The comparison uses 300M and 500M models, TPP
10–80, and L ∈{2, 4}. The ordering broadly matches DCLM: unshifted PLT is strongest, while shifted PLT generally
improves over Full-Stack Looping and retains parallel decoding. Lower BPB is better.

Figure 135 Parallel looping with Loop Matrix on RedPajama and Dolma programming languages. The top figure shows
RedPajama and the bottom figure shows the code-heavy Dolma programming-languages split.
